Pick the NVLOG path, and make TPG and relative target port IDs RSM-assigned constants so both nodes present one device with stable port identities across takeover.

6. **IPMI is not a fence rung.** "iDRAC9 Redfish/IPMI for fencing": IPMI over LAN is disabled by default on current iDRAC9 firmware (DSA-2024-099), and if the iDRAC shares the failed network neither works; the watchdog bound is the only fence that is always available, so the design must tolerate the 8 s floor.

#### Cross-cutting conflicts (Opus-Consensus)
1. **Three record formats.** Opus-Network: 64 B header + trailer CRC, slot-addressed, `req_id`. Opus-Storage: 4 KiB-aligned `RecHdr` with `cp_epoch`, no owner epoch, `append -> Seq` RPC. Opus-Security: `{epoch, seq, obj, offset, len, crc32c, data}`. Opus-Consensus needs `(aggregate_id, owner_epoch, nvlog_seq, cp_seq)` plus a CP boundary. Alignment alone changes the mirror link's 4 KiB record ceiling by 2x. One owner, one format, before Phase 1 code.

2. **Mirror semantics.** Opus-Network's write-only ring (BlockRing cannot reject) vs Opus-Storage's request/response append, Opus-Security's `fence_epoch` (\"partner refuses frames\") and Opus-Consensus's \"ACK carries the applied epoch, stale sender fails within one RTT\". Either FC accepts lease-only fencing and inline rejection is dropped as a Phase 1 criterion, or the FC receiver must respond (TCMU over out-of-tree `tcm_qla2xxx`).

3. **Unmirrored precondition.** Opus-Network: partner fenced. Opus-Storage: owner's policy. Opus-Consensus: quorum-committed grant that disables takeover. Mutually exclusive, and the mirror-link-down row of the partition matrix comes out differently under each.

4. **Takeover time.** Opus-Consensus 6-14 s (12-16 s without a confirmed fence); Opus-Security 10 s target, 25 s ceiling; Opus-Storage 10-30 s with replay alone 15-30 s. At most one holds; continuous partner-side indexing is the only change under which all three fit.

5. **Raft over the mirror transport** (Opus-Consensus) vs BlockRing's write-only semantics and lpfc's target-or-initiator ports: either Raft and heartbeats get their own channel, or FC heartbeats are polled slots with no RPC.

6. **Shadow replica** (Opus-Storage) vs the PR rung of Opus-Consensus's fence ladder and Opus-Security's \"PR + STONITH before assemble\": shadow mode has no device-enforced fence and makes the witness mandatory; it also replaces Opus-Network's \"the mirror link is the ceiling\" with \"the partner's array is the ceiling\".

7. **FC HBA family and tier.** Opus-Security's QLogic/`tcm_qla2xxx` vs Opus-Network's Emulex/`nvmet-fc`; one chassis cannot do both without two card families. The brief's \"FC primary goal\" vs all four specialists saying FC is an isolation tier, not a latency tier.

8. **Process topology.** Opus-Storage: one process per aggregate. Opus-Security: N and D in one binary. Opus-Consensus: separate `otap-ctl` plus a shared epoch page. Restart semantics, watchdog petting and lease ownership all depend on which it is.

9. **Failure detectors.** Opus-Network 20 ms heartbeats, dead at 1 s, `T_stall` 5 s; Opus-Consensus 100 ms, silent at 2 s, lease 3 s, watchdog 5 s. Exactly one may decide anything.

10. **Takeover granularity.** Per aggregate (Opus-Consensus, Opus-Storage) vs per LUN `(lun, epoch)` (Opus-Security) vs per volume `gen` plus mirror `epoch` (Opus-Network). Compatible only if LUN and volume inherit the aggregate's epoch and `gen` is deferred.

#### Questions for the lead architect (Opus-Consensus)
1. **Witness:** is a third voter (VM or management host) allowed, on a network path independent of the cluster switches? If refused, is a JBOD disk arbiter or manual failover acceptable for v1?
2. **Disks:** external dual-EMM SAS JBOD per pair (SAS/NL-SAS only, HBA355e, PR fencing), shadow replica (halves ingest, witness mandatory, no device fence), or node-local with manual failover? This must precede the record format.
3. **NVLOG medium:** NVDIMM-N with battery (no 2.5-inch mid-tray), Optane PMem (Cascade Lake only), or PLP NVMe? Is RPO=0 required across a rack-level power event?
4. **Mirror-down policy:** block writes, unmirrored-with-committed-grant, or a shared SAS-SSD journal in the JBOD?
5. **FC:** client fabric only, or also the mirror? May the appliance ship out-of-tree modules or its own kernel? Emulex (NVMe/FC in-box) or QLogic (SCSI target, out-of-tree), or both families?
6. **Takeover stall budget** (10, 20, 30 s) and initiator population (ESXi, Windows MPIO, Linux, NVMe/TCP); may we require initiator timer tuning?
7. **Record format ownership:** who owns the single record format, and is the aggregate-scoped owner epoch the only fence token in v1?
8. **Process topology:** separate control, N-Blade and D-Blade processes? How many D-Blades per node, and what must survive a daemon restart without an initiator-visible stall?
9. **Baseline:** Rocky 10.1 (io_uring, ublk) as the floor, or must Rocky 9 be supported?
10. **1GbE tier:** a deliverable, or may Phase 1 start on 10GbE so batching, pinning and the 15 s takeover budget are measured on something that transfers?

### By Opus-Security

#### Opus-Security on Opus-Network
**1. BlockRing fencing is wrong on both legs.** Proposal: `can_reject=false`, "stale-epoch records are rejected at replay", "ACL removal within 1 s". (a) Every record the legitimate owner wrote before takeover carries the *same* epoch as what a zombie writes after it, so replay cannot separate them by epoch: it either discards acknowledged writes (RPO breach) or admits zombies. (b) I read 6.12 [`drivers/nvme/target/configfs.c`](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/drivers/nvme/target/configfs.c): `nvmet_allowed_hosts_drop_link` only calls `nvmet_subsys_disc_changed`; live controllers keep writing. Only `nvmet_port_subsys_drop_link` -> `nvmet_port_del_ctrls` kills the association. Instead: the BlockRing fence is "unlink subsystem from port, then replay everything that CRC-verifies", strictly before the survivor serves anything; the record epoch exists for the *next* owner's writes, not for filtering replay. Make that the success criterion.

**2. "Zero copies NIC->arena" cannot be demonstrated with the front door they chose.** With LIO/nvmet + ublk on 6.12 the kernel copies socket->bio page and bio page->ublk buffer (`ublk_copy_user_pages`); the daemon only chooses *where* the second copy lands. Receiving iSCSI/NVMe-TCP data segments "directly into an arena slot" presumes a userspace protocol stack nobody builds before a native NVMe target, and multishot provided-buffer recv cannot steer a PDU's data segment to a chosen slot anyway. Instead: criterion "payload lands once in the arena (ublk buffers *are* arena slots); mirror and CP read the same pages via SEND_ZC/WRITE_FIXED"; revisit at 6.15 ublk zero-copy.

**3. The "dual-use Rust TCMU handler, ~1.5K lines" is a 20K-line project.** TCMU is a passthrough backstore: target core does not emulate SBC/SPC for it, so the handler must implement INQUIRY plus VPD 0x80/0x83/0x86/0xB0/0xB2, MODE SENSE/SELECT, READ CAPACITY(16), SYNCHRONIZE CACHE, UNMAP, WRITE SAME, COMPARE AND WRITE (VMFS ATS), XCOPY (VAAI), and RTPG if ALUA is passed through. That is what tcmu-runner is; ESXi/Windows interop on it is months. It also puts a single-ring, single-lock TCMU device (~100-200K IOPS) behind every LUN. Instead: no Rocky 9 front door at all; Rocky 10 + ublk, TCMU later only for cluster-PR LUNs.

**4. The stall budget ignores what LIO and nvmet do to held I/O.** "Initiators tolerate 20-30 s" is the *guest* timeout. Linux initiators send ABORT TASK at 30 s; the NVMe/TCP host resets the whole controller at `io_timeout`=30 s, tearing down every queue; LIO's ABORT TASK waits on ublk, which has no per-IO abort, so a long stall escalates to LUN RESET -> session drop -> path failover on every initiator at once. Instead: cap stall+takeover at ~15 s, and on entering Stalled flip ALUA to Transitioning / ANA Change so *new* commands get retryable sense instead of queuing behind un-abortable ublk I/O.

**5. `req_id` plus a 1K-entry per-session dedupe window in the D-Blade is state for a "stateless" N-Blade that buys nothing.** WRITE/DISCARD/WRITE ZEROES are idempotent; a session reinstated after N-Blade loss carries a new session epoch, so the window never matches; the non-idempotent ops (CAW, PR OUT, XCOPY) never traverse ublk. Instead: drop `req_id` from the frozen record; model CAW/PR as explicit D-Blade log ops when a passthrough path exists.

#### Opus-Security on Opus-Storage
**1. `op: Caw` and "COMPARE AND WRITE executes atomically under the shard lock" have no delivery path.** ublk carries only Read/Write/Flush/Discard/WriteZeroes. LIO executes CAW in `target_core_sbc` (read-then-write under `caw_sem`, per node), XCOPY in `target_core_xcopy`, PR in `target_core_pr`, all above the backstore. Until a TCMU passthrough handler or a native NVMe target exists the D-Blade never sees a CAW, so VMFS ATS atomicity is LIO's and per node, which is exactly why ALUA must stay Active/Standby. Instead: v1 record ops = Write/Discard/WriteZeroes (+Snap/Clone); CAW/PR become a Phase 3 item coupled to the front-door decision.

**2. "CRC computed at the edge" and the open sector/discard questions need front-door facts.** In Phase 1 the edge is the ublk buffer after two kernel copies; wire integrity is iSCSI Header/DataDigest and NVMe/TCP DDGST, which initiators negotiate and ESXi, Windows and open-iscsi default to none, so "end-to-end" starts at ublk and policy must prefer CRC32C digests in LIO/nvmet. 4Kn: ESXi supports 4Kn only as local disks (I believe), so SAN LUNs must be 512e; ublk `logical_bs_shift=9/physical_bs_shift=12` makes LIO's READ CAPACITY(16) report lbppbe=3 so guests align. Discard: LIO derives `emulate_tpu`/UNMAP limits from the ublk discard parameters and reports LBPRZ from `unmap_zeroes_data` (I believe), so advertising it obliges the map to return zeros after discard at 4 KiB granularity. Instead: fold these into the ublk parameter spec now.

**3. The mirror semantic ("append, get `Seq` back", `Gap`, partner CRC-checks/ACKs/`retire`) requires a receiving daemon on the ACK path**, which the only FC design on the table (Network's BlockRing) lacks; and the FC variant proposed here (LIO fileio ring over tcm_qla2xxx) is out-of-tree on Rocky: CentOS Stream 9 and 10 configs both carry `# CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX is not set`, per [Bugzilla 1666377](https://bugzilla.redhat.com/show_bug.cgi?id=1666377). The shadow replica is called "free" but halves sustainable ingest (the partner array absorbs 2x writes: ~0.35-0.45 GB/s net per 12-HDD node), which the throughput section omits. Instead: one ring model (slot-addressed, receiver-optional) and the shadow's bandwidth in the budget.

**4. "The D-Blade runs on both; Rocky 9 is acceptable with the sysctl and SELinux boolean" is a non-decision.** The pair runs one kernel; the N-Blade needs ublk (absent from 5.14), nvmet DH-HMAC-CHAP/TLS (6.0/6.7) and io_uring multishot/SENDZC (6.0). Choosing 9 for the D-Blade forces the N-Blade onto TCMU (Network #3). Instead: Rocky 10.1 only.

**5. Latency criteria are 2-3x looser than the other proposals for the same hop** (D-Blade ACK p50 <=150 / p99 <=500 us; "client-observed 200-400 us") versus Network's p50 <=60 us and my 90-150 us client end-to-end. At 200-400 us the QD1 story is 2.5-5K IOPS, which no longer reads as "network speed". Instead: one owner for the latency table.

#### Opus-Security on Opus-Consensus
**1. The takeover timeline mistakes the target's clock for the client's.** "9-12 s stall, inside ESXi's 10 s RecoveryTimeout": RecoveryTimeout runs *after* ESXi notices the session is gone, and a ForceOff'd node sends no FIN/RST, so detection is NOP-driven: NoopOutInterval 15 s + NoopOutTimeout 10 s + RecoveryTimeout 10 s = a 25-35 s pause with ESXi defaults, regardless of how fast the fence ladder runs ([Dell KB 000418707](https://www.dell.com/support/kbdoc/en-au/000418707/esxi-hosts-using-iscsi-protocol)); open-iscsi is ~10 s of NOPs then `replacement_timeout`; Windows ~15-30 s. Instead: make the "optional" floating IP mandatory for iSCSI and do it properly: the survivor raises the dead node's portal IP and hosts its target IQN under a second TPGT, so retransmits hit RST and initiators re-login by session reinstatement inside RecoveryTimeout; the client stall then equals the fence ladder, not the NOP cycle. Alternatively publish host tuning (NoopOutInterval 5) as NetApp Host Utilities do. NVMe/TCP (KATO + ANA AEN on the surviving path) is the fast case; showcase it. Also Windows guest disk timeout is 60 s, not 20.

**2. "PR, UAs and ALUA state live in the D-Blade or RSM" has no mechanism while LIO fronts the LUN.** PR OUT executes in `target_core_pr` on the receiving node; nothing in configfs injects registrations; APTPL files are per node and written only when the initiator asks. TCMU with `pgr_support=0` delivers PR CDBs to userspace, but then the handler must run the RESERVATION CONFLICT check on *every* command; nvmet reservations are post-6.12. WSFC renews reservations about every 3 s per LUN (I believe), so routing PR OUT through Raft breaks "a few commits per minute". Instead: v1 states "PR/UA not preserved across takeover"; the RSM holds only *identity* (NAA/serial, TPGT and relative port IDs, LUN numbers, ACLs); PR goes into the D-Blade log once a passthrough path exists.

**3. Takeover in 15 s needs pre-staged standby exports, which the RSM and the takeover gate lack.** ESXi does not rescan on its own; a LUN created at takeover is invisible for minutes. Every LUN must already be exported by the survivor (same IQN/NQN, same `vpd_unit_serial`/NGUID, same LUN number, distinct TPGT/ANA group, Standby/Inaccessible) over a ublk device whose daemon refuses I/O by epoch. Instead: a `luns[id]` table in the RSM, a two-node applier, and `BeginTakeover` gated on "standby staged and healthy".

**4. Control-plane trust is unaddressed.** The witness is a full voter: whoever owns it can commit `GrantUnmirrored` or `BeginTakeover`; iDRAC credentials sit on every node; `/dev/watchdog` and SG_IO need root. Instead: a cluster CA issuing per-node certificates at join, mTLS on Raft and on the epoch feed, witness on the management VLAN only, an iDRAC account with the power-control role only, secrets root-only or TPM-sealed.

**5. CONSENSUS-3 is now verified, and the consequence is broader than the mirror.** Bugzilla 1666377: "Management decided to disable qla2xxx target support in RHEL8"; CentOS Stream 9 and 10 kernel configs both carry `# CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX is not set`. That also removes FC-SCSI *client* targets (ESXi/Windows over FC) from a stock Rocky kernel; only Emulex NVMe/FC remains in-box, and Windows has no NVMe/FC path worth shipping. Instead: the decision record must choose "ship our own kernel module" versus "FC means NVMe/FC only".

#### Cross-cutting conflicts (Opus-Security)
- **Record and ring semantics (all four).** Opus-Network: slot-addressed, unordered, receiver-optional, 64 B header + trailer, `req_id`. Opus-Storage: stream append returning `Seq`, `Gap` detection, partner CRC+ACK+`retire`, 40 B `RecHdr` with `vol: u16` and no owner epoch. Opus-Consensus: `(aggregate_id, owner_epoch, nvlog_seq, cp_seq)` with in-line stale-epoch rejection and an ACK carrying the applied epoch. Mine: `{epoch, seq, obj, offset, len, crc32c}` with `fence_epoch`. In-line epoch rejection (Storage, Consensus, me) and a receiver-less FC ring (Network) cannot both be the mirror contract; FC either gets a receiving daemon or everyone accepts \"fence by port unlink, not by epoch\" on that transport.\n- **Unmirrored policy.** Network: never ACK unmirrored until the partner is fenced (power-off confirmed, or lease expiry + witness). Consensus: the `M down, E up` row grants Unmirrored with the partner alive, takeover disabled. Both cannot be the rule.\n- **FC.** I and Storage build on tcm_qla2xxx (client FC for free; fileio ring); Network on Emulex nvmet-fc; Consensus on \"demote or custom kernel\". With `# CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX is not set` verified on CentOS Stream 9 and 10, my own \"FC client fabric for free\" claim and Storage's ring fall unless we ship a module.\n- **Latency budget.** Three incompatible success criteria for one hop: Network p50 <=60 us mirror RTT; Storage p50 <=150 / p99 <=500 us D-Blade ACK and 200-400 us client; me <=150 us client end-to-end.\n- **Core budget.** Network pins a core per mirror link plus RPC links; Storage pins ingest/NVLOG/CP threads per core plus md group threads; I pin NIC RSS <-> LIO <-> ublk <-> shard 1:1:1; Consensus keeps core 0 free. Nobody summed them for a 2x16-core SKU.\n- **Zero copy vs ublk.** Network's zero-copy criterion versus the ublk front door (mine and theirs) on 6.12.\n- **Baseline.** Storage accepts Rocky 9; Network and I require 10.\n- **Mirror link sizing.** Network: the link must exceed array flush rate (2x10GbE/25GbE); me: one 10GbE balances 12 HDDs and 25GbE starves them; Storage: ingest exceeds the array either way.\n- **Raft transport.** Consensus rides Raft on the mirror `Transport` (send/recv, my trait shape); Network's `RemoteRing` has no receive side on FC, so Raft-over-FC is a polled mailbox protocol, a second implementation.

#### Questions for the lead architect (Opus-Security)
1. Shared dual-ported SAS JBOD, shadow replica, or a written manual-failover statement? Decides the ring consumer, allocator importability, SAS-only purchase, and whether ALUA takeover is real in Phase 1.\n2. May the appliance ship its own kernel or out-of-tree modules? If not, FC is Emulex NVMe/FC only, there are no FC-SCSI clients, and the FC mirror ring is receiver-less.\n3. Is Rocky 10.1 the sole baseline (ublk, io_uring defaults, nvmet auth/TLS)?\n4. NVLOG medium (NVDIMM-N, Optane PMem, PLP NVMe, DRAM prototype), and is RPO=0 required under dual power loss?\n5. Unmirrored rule: fence-first (Network) or Raft grant with takeover disabled (Consensus)?\n6. Who owns the record format and who owns the latency table? Which numbers become the Phase 1 contract?\n7. Initiator population and acceptable stall: tune hosts (35 s -> ~20 s), migrate portals to the survivor, or NVMe-first?\n8. Must persistent reservations survive takeover in v1 (WSFC, in-guest clustering)? If yes, TCMU passthrough enters Phase 1-3 scope.\n9. Is a witness VM allowed, and is iDRAC on a physically separate network?\n10. Per-node core count and NIC count to budget pinned cores against?

## Fact-check verdicts (32 claims)

### [NETWORK-1] CONFIRMED (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: The RHEL 9 and RHEL 10 x86_64 kernel configs (Rocky r9/r10 kernel-x86_64-rhel.config) contain '# CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX is not set' and '# CONFIG_SCSI_EFCT is not set', so no in-box FC SCSI target module exists for QLogic or Emulex HBAs on Rocky 9 or 10.
- Evidence: Fetched Rocky's kernel-x86_64-rhel.config directly. r9 branch (5.14-based): "# CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX is not set", "# CONFIG_SCSI_EFCT is not set", CONFIG_TCM_FC=m, CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FC=m, CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FCLOOP=m, CONFIG_SCSI_QLA_FC=m, CONFIG_SCSI_LPFC=m, CONFIG_TARGET_CORE=m. r10 branch (6.12-based): identical for all of these. Sources: https://git.rockylinux.org/staging/rpms/kernel/-/raw/r9/SOURCES/kernel-x86_64-rhel.config and https://git.rockylinux.org/staging/rpms/kernel/-/raw/r10/SOURCES/kernel-x86_64-rhel.config. The only LIO FC fabric module shipped is tcm_fc, which is the libfc/FCoE target (software FCoE over Ethernet), not native FC on QLogic/Emulex HBAs, so the claim's conclusion holds. Caveats: (1) Red Hat's RHEL 9 storage guide says NVMe/FC is "fully supported in host mode" only (https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/9/html/managing_storage_devices/configuring-nvme-over-fabrics-using-nvme-fc_managing-storage-devices), so nvmet-fc+lpfc target mode is in-box but a Red Hat-unsupported configuration. (2) linux-nvme list, April 2025 (Reinecke/Wagner): qla2xxx has no nvmet-fc target support and Marvell has no plan to add it; lpfc target works via lpfc_enable_nvmet=<WWPNs> (https://lists.infradead.org/pipermail/linux-nvme/2025-April/055140.html). (3) Broadcom lists LPe32000/LPe35000 families for target systems. (4) RHEL's qla2xxx.ko still compiles qla_target.c, so an out-of-tree tcm_qla2xxx is a single small module built against kernel-devel, not a whole kernel; but its qlt_* symbols are not kABI-stable, so it needs rebuild per kernel update plus Secure Boot signing, and RHEL 9.4+ disables older QLogic PCI IDs (8Gb ISP24xx/25xx).
- Design impact: The nvmet-fc-on-Emulex recommendation stands: buy LPe32000/LPe35000-class HBAs, enable target mode per WWPN via lpfc_enable_nvmet, and accept that Red Hat treats FC target mode as unsupported (relevant for FC SCSI clients too: there is no in-box native FC SCSI target at all, so FC SCSI hosts would need an out-of-tree tcm_qla2xxx/DKMS path or SCST). If QLogic is kept, budget for a DKMS-built, signed tcm_qla2xxx module re-built on every kernel update, and avoid 8Gb-era QLogic cards disabled by RHEL 9.4+.

### [NETWORK-2] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: The same RHEL 9 and RHEL 10 configs build CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FC=m (plus _TCP and _RDMA), and in-tree lpfc compiles its NVMe target support (lpfc_enable_nvmet) whenever NVME_TARGET_FC is enabled, so an Emulex LPe31002/LPe32002 can act as an in-box NVMe/FC target on Rocky 9 or 10.
- Correction: Technically true, with caveats. Rocky 9 (5.14) and Rocky 10 (6.12) kernel configs both carry CONFIG_NVME_TARGET=m, _FC=m, _TCP=m, _RDMA=m, _LOOP=m, _FCLOOP=m and CONFIG_SCSI_LPFC=m, and lpfc's target path (lpfc_sli4_driver_resource_setup WWPN match -> phba->nvmet_support=1; nvmet_fc_register_targetport in lpfc_nvmet.c) is compiled in under #if IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FC), still present in v6.12. So a LPe31002/LPe32002 (SLI-4) can be put in NVMe/FC target mode on Rocky 9/10 via lpfc_enable_nvmet=<WWPN list>. But: (1) Red Hat explicitly states "Red Hat does not support the NVMe Target (nvmet) functionality" and that "HBAs operating in target mode are not supported for NVMe/FC"; nvmet_fc tainting/Tech-Preview history applies. (2) Target mode is NVMe/FC only; lpfc has no SCSI/FCP (LIO) target, so no in-box FC SCSI target exists for Emulex. (3) A port is exclusively target or initiator, selected at module load by WWPN; nvmet_fc also requires lpfc_enable_fc4_type to include NVMe and firmware that supports target mode. (4) Rocky rebuilds RHEL configs, so expect parity but verify /boot/config on the installed kernel.
- Evidence: Rocky r9 and r10 kernel configs (https://git.rockylinux.org/staging/rpms/kernel/-/raw/r9/SOURCES/kernel-x86_64-rhel.config and .../r10/...): CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FC=m, _TCP=m, _RDMA=m, CONFIG_SCSI_LPFC=m. CentOS Stream 9/10 redhat/configs/common/generic/CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FC = m. Upstream v6.12 lpfc_init.c and lpfc_nvmet.c: IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_NVME_TARGET_FC) guards around nvmet_support and nvmet_fc_register_targetport, else "6021 Can't enable NVME Target... not in kernel". Original Kconfig patch: https://lore.kernel.org/all/yq137egr1wm.fsf@oracle.com/T/. Red Hat support position: https://access.redhat.com/solutions/3522911 (target mode not supported for NVMe/FC; nvmet_fc Tech Preview/taint) and https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/9/html/managing_storage_devices/configuring-nvme-over-fabrics-using-nvme-tcp_managing-storage-devices ("Red Hat does not support the NVMe Target (nvmet) functionality").
- Design impact: BlockRing-over-FC can proceed on in-box modules, but OpenTAP owns all support risk for nvmet_fc+lpfc target mode (no Red Hat backing; kernel taint), and must treat it as NVMe/FC-only: FC ports dedicated to the mirror link are target-or-initiator, not both, and no in-box SCSI/FCP target over Emulex exists if a legacy FC front end is ever wanted. Plan a lab validation of lpfc target firmware/parameters on the actual LPe31002/32002 cards before committing.

### [NETWORK-3] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: A write to a file on an fsdax filesystem backed by pmem completes through the DAX copy path (copy_from_iter_flushcache, non-temporal stores plus flush), so an nvmet file-backed namespace on fsdax acknowledges an NVMe write only after the data is in the persistence domain, without any userspace receiver.
- Correction: The mechanism is real: nvmet's file backend opens the file O_RDWR|O_LARGEFILE|O_DIRECT (no FMODE_CAN_ODIRECT/direct_IO check in either 5.14 or 6.12), and both xfs_file_write_iter and ext4_file_write_iter test IS_DAX(inode) before IOCB_DIRECT, so the write goes to dax_iomap_rw -> _copy_from_iter_flushcache (movnti + clwb for unaligned head/tail). But "acknowledges only after the data is durable" holds only under conditions: (1) the file must be fully pre-written, not merely fallocated: in XFS an unwritten extent is converted in xfs_iomap_write_direct by a transaction that is committed to the in-memory log but not forced, and ext4 block allocation / i_size extension likewise needs a journal commit, so a crash after the ack can leave the extent unwritten and the LBA reads back zeros even though bytes reached pmem; (2) the platform must have ADR (persistence_domain = memory_controller or cpu_cache); no WPQ/deep flush occurs on this path; (3) neither memcpy_flushcache nor dax_iomap_iter issues an sfence after the non-temporal stores; ordering before the completion is only incidental (locked instructions in the completion path), not an explicit guarantee. Setting FUA on the replication write fixes (1) and (3): nvmet maps NVME_RW_FUA to IOCB_DSYNC -> generic_write_sync -> fsync range -> log force + blkdev flush -> nvdimm_flush. NVMe Flush maps to vfs_fsync(datasync=1). Rocky 9 (5.14) additionally cannot mount XFS with dax if reflink is enabled (mkfs -m reflink=0 required); Rocky 10 (6.12) allows DAX+reflink.
- Evidence: drivers/nvme/target/io-cmd-file.c v5.14 and v6.12 (O_DIRECT open, FUA->IOCB_DSYNC, flush->vfs_fsync): https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/drivers/nvme/target/io-cmd-file.c ; fs/xfs/xfs_file.c and fs/ext4/file.c v6.12 (IS_DAX checked before IOCB_DIRECT; generic_write_sync after dax_iomap_rw); fs/xfs/xfs_iomap.c (imap_needs_alloc for IOMAP_DAX on unwritten -> xfs_iomap_write_direct, xfs_trans_commit, no log force); fs/dax.c dax_iomap_iter (no fence/flush after copy); arch/x86/lib/usercopy_64.c (__memcpy_flushcache: movnti + clwb, no sfence); drivers/nvdimm/pmem.c (nvdimm_flush only on REQ_PREFLUSH/FUA); persistence_domain semantics: https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/1803.2/06259.html ; RHEL DAX+reflink KB: https://access.redhat.com/node/3711211
- Design impact: BlockRing's file-on-fsdax receiver can still be daemon-free, but the backing file must be fully written once at provisioning (dd/zero fill, not fallocate) so no metadata changes occur on overwrite, and the sender should set FUA on each replication write (cheap on pmem: a journal commit plus nvdimm_flush) if 'Durable' is to mean crash-readable rather than merely 'bytes reached ADR domain'. Verify persistence_domain on the R740xd's DCPMM config, and on Rocky 9 format the XFS with reflink=0.

### [NETWORK-4] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: RHEL 9.4 and later ship kernel.io_uring_disabled=2 by default (io_uring_setup fails with EPERM), while RHEL 10 builds ublk as a module (CONFIG_BLK_DEV_UBLK=m) and RHEL 9's 5.14-based kernel has no ublk at all.
- Correction: All three facts are right, but the framing omits the decisive caveat: RHEL 10 (10.0, 10.1) ALSO ships kernel.io_uring_disabled=2 by default and labels io_uring "Technology Preview" (unsupported). So moving to Rocky 10 does not make io_uring/ublk work out of the box; the sysctl must be set to 0 (everyone) or 1 (CAP_SYS_ADMIN or io_uring_group members) on either release. Precise statement: RHEL 9.4+ and RHEL 10.x both default io_uring_disabled=2 (io_uring_setup -> EPERM); RHEL 9.0-9.3 had CONFIG_IO_URING=y with no such sysctl, so io_uring was usable there. RHEL 10's 6.12 kernel has CONFIG_BLK_DEV_UBLK=m (with 2025 ublk CVE fixes backported under RHEL-106845); RHEL 9's 5.14 kernel config has no CONFIG_BLK_DEV_UBLK at all. ublk's userspace server itself runs on io_uring, so ublk on Rocky 10 is gated by the same sysctl.
- Evidence: RHEL 9.4 release notes, sysctl changes: io_uring_disabled default 2, io_uring_setup fails -EPERM, plus io_uring_group (https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/9/html/9.4_release_notes/kernel_parameters_changes). RHEL 10.0 release notes: same io_uring_disabled=2 default in "Updated sysctl parameters", and Technology Preview chapter says io_uring "is disabled by default in RHEL 10" (https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/10/html/10.0_release_notes/technology-preview-features; same in 10.1). Jira RHEL-65347 "io_uring will ship as tech preview in 10.0". AlmaLinux el10 kernel 6.12.0-130 commit shows CONFIG_BLK_DEV_UBLK=m in kernel-x86_64-rhel.config and ~100 ublk changelog entries (https://git.almalinux.org/rpms/kernel/commit/f170c0ad568be886aee8d914037d6888c1ed81dc). AlmaLinux a9 kernel-x86_64-rhel.config: CONFIG_IO_URING=y, zero "UBLK" lines across the whole file (https://git.almalinux.org/rpms/kernel/raw/branch/a9/SOURCES/kernel-x86_64-rhel.config). Rocky 9.3 forum thread confirms io_uring worked on a stock 9.3 host (https://forums.rockylinux.org/t/io-uring-support-rocky-9-3/12384).
- Design impact: Rocky 10 is still the right base (ublk exists only there), but the Phase 1 install must ship a sysctl.d drop-in setting kernel.io_uring_disabled=1 (run the ublk/io_uring daemons as root or in io_uring_group) or 0, and must treat io_uring as Red Hat-unsupported Technology Preview on both 9 and 10. Do not assume io_uring is unavailable on Rocky 9.0-9.3 or available on Rocky 10 without that change; add a startup probe that fails loudly on EPERM.

### [NETWORK-5] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: A 4 KiB request/ack round trip over kernel TCP on 10GbE is roughly 40-55 us interrupt-driven and 20-30 us busy-polled, versus 5-7 us for an RDMA write-with-immediate on 25GbE ConnectX-5 and 50-150 us for an FC write through a Linux kernel target stack; serialization alone is 32.8 us per 4 KiB on 1GbE, 3.3 us on 10GbE, 2.6 us on 16GFC (14.025 Gbit/s).
- Correction: Serialization: 4 KiB = 32,768 bits → 32.8 us at 1GbE and 3.28 us at 10GbE (payload only; add ~5% headers, and three 1500-byte frames unless jumbo). The 16GFC figure is mislabeled: at the stated 14.025 Gbaud line rate it is 2.34 us; 2.6 us corresponds to FCIA's 1,600 MB/s usable payload rate (64b/66b plus framing). Kernel-TCP RTT is not a fixed 40-55 us: with coalescing off and C-states pinned, 1-byte TCP_RR is ~16-21 us interrupt-driven and ~9 us busy-polled on 10GbE; with adaptive/100 us rx-usecs it reaches ~100 us. The claimed bands are mid-range values of a tuning-dominated spread (~15-100 us), not properties of TCP. RDMA 5-7 us for a 4 KiB write+completion on ConnectX-5/25GbE is plausible (ib_write_lat ~1.5-2 us one-way small-message, +1.3 us serialization; note perftest reports half the RTT). FC 50-150 us is plausible but apples-to-oranges: it includes LIO/tcm_qla2xxx target processing and FCP's mandatory XFER_RDY (two fabric round trips per write unless first-burst, which LIO FC does not offer), whereas the TCP figure is a bare socket ping-pong; LIO iSCSI on 10GbE lands in the same 50-100 us band. Rocky caveats: FC target mode (tcm_qla2xxx) is not a Red Hat-supported configuration; RHEL 9 lists nvmet-fc and several qla2xxx IDs as unmaintained; io_uring NAPI busy polling exists only in 6.9+ (Rocky 10's 6.12), not Rocky 9's 5.14 (SO_BUSY_POLL/epoll only).
- Evidence: Chelsio cxgb4 busy-poll commit (TCP_RR 16.25 us → 8.79 us): https://android-kvm.googlesource.com/linux/+/3a336cb17183b29827fdffaffb5e62f8912f5ca1 ; 2015 netdev thread (9.7k→42k RR/s, X520, rx-usecs=100): https://lore.kernel.ime.usp.br/netdev/5508AA13.3030401@in.tum.de/ ; Hurwitz & Feng 21 us app-to-app 10GbE: https://cs.uwaterloo.ca/~brecht/servers/readings-new/HotI11_Hurwitz_10GbE.pdf ; FCIA speedmap 16GFC 14.025 Gbaud / 1,600 MBps: https://fibrechannel.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/FCIA-Speedmap-V24-July-2023.pdf ; FCP write XFER_RDY mandatory / first-burst: https://www.t10.org/ftp/t10/document.00/00-377r0.pdf ; RHEL 9 unmaintained (nvmet-fc, qla2xxx IDs): https://access.redhat.com/documentation/vi-vn/red_hat_enterprise_linux/9/html/considerations_in_adopting_rhel_9/assembly_hardware-enablement_considerations-in-adopting-rhel-9 ; io_uring_register_napi (6.9+): https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man3/io_uring_register_napi.3.html ; LIO FC target: https://linux-iscsi.org/?p=405. No published ConnectX-5 25GbE 4 KiB ib_write_lat found; RDMA band is derived.
- Design impact: The ordering RDMA < tuned TCP < FC-through-LIO holds, so FC still is not the latency tier; but the 2-3x TCP band is a tuning artifact (coalescing, C-states, busy poll), so OpenTAP must pin rx-usecs/C-states and use busy polling (io_uring NAPI on Rocky 10) rather than size queue depth from 40-55 us. FC mirroring has a second problem beyond latency: it would ride an unsupported-on-RHEL kernel target path (tcm_qla2xxx, or unmaintained nvmet-fc) with no userspace datapath, which should be weighed before committing to FC as the phase-2 interconnect.

### [NETWORK-6] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: qla2xxx supports per-port qlini_mode 'dual' (initiator and target on one port) and direct-connect N2N target mode in mainline kernels since about 5.5 (with a dual-mode fix in 5.15), but N2N target mode has needed repeated fixes and dual-mode N2N between two Linux qla2xxx ports, and lpfc nvmet target in point-to-point topology, are unproven.
- Correction: qla2xxx has had a module-wide qlini_mode=dual since 4.13 (2017) and per-port sysfs mode control (enabled/disabled/dual/exclusive, driver must load with qlini_mode=dual) since 5.2 via commit 0645cb83 (Apr 2019), not 5.5. N2N (direct-connect) login support landed in 4.15 (edd05de19759, Oct 2017); N2N *target-mode* login became workable with Bolshakov's fixes in 5.5 (fd1de5830a5a, "Allow PLOGI in target mode"). The "dual-mode fix in 5.15" is correct: "Restore initiator in dual mode" (5f857903, applied to 5.15/scsi-fixes). N2N fixes continued: NVMe connect retry (5.10 era), "Prevent PRLI in target mode" (5.12), "Fix N2N inconsistent PLOGI" (5.16/5.17), EDIF N2N (5.19), and a Nov-2025 16-patch "target mode improvements" series not in 6.12. lpfc nvmet in Pt2Pt is not "unproven": it is an explicitly coded, Broadcom-maintained path (4.14 "Fix handling of FCP and NVME FC4 types in Pt2Pt", 7f4c5a26f735 "Fix pt2pt NVMe PRLI reject LOGO loop", Cc stable v5.4+), though no public vendor support statement for NVMe target direct-attach was found. Linux-to-Linux dual-mode N2N with both ends qla2xxx has no public evidence either way. Rocky 9 (5.14 + RHEL driver rebases to 10.02.09.x) and Rocky 10 (6.12) both carry all the pre-2024 fixes.
- Evidence: Per-port mode commit 0645cb83: https://github.com/torvalds/linux/commit/0645cb8350cdb60bfbf91caa722984b81c215add. Restore-initiator fix (Fixes 0645cb83, 5.15/scsi-fixes): https://github.com/torvalds/linux/commit/5f8579038842d77e6ce05e1df6bf9dd493b0e3ef. N2N logins (edd05de19759): https://git.evolution-x.org/anierin/msm-5.15/commit/edd05de197592. N2N target local-loop fix fd1de5830a5a (4.19 backport, Jan 2020): https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/2001.0/05264.html. Bolshakov series: https://lore.kernel.org/all/yq1blshqdf7.fsf@oracle.com/T/. Prevent PRLI in target mode (5.12): https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/2105.2/01259.html. N2N inconsistent PLOGI: https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/2204.0/05959.html. lpfc pt2pt NVMe: https://patchwork.kernel.org/comment/20870749, https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/2202.2/05901.html. Nov-2025 target-mode series: https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/2511.1/02204.html. Version dates slightly off but the substantive risk picture holds.
- Design impact: Direct-connect FC between the HA pair is plausible on both Rocky kernels but is a bug-churn path with no public Linux-to-Linux dual-mode N2N validation; treat it as a lab-qualify item with a fallback (two cheap FC switches, or keeping Ethernet as the mirror link) rather than a committed Tier-2 dependency. If choosing lpfc for NVMe target, the pt2pt path exists and is vendor-maintained, so it is a comparable rather than worse bet.

### [NETWORK-7] CONFIRMED (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: Skylake/Cascade Lake Xeons with NVDIMM-N provide ADR but not eADR, so data in CPU caches (including DDIO-targeted LLC for inbound RDMA/NIC writes) is not persistent until CLWB/CLFLUSHOPT plus SFENCE; the R740xd supports at most 12 x 16 GB NVDIMM-N with a battery and two CPUs.
- Evidence: ADR vs eADR: pmem.io glossary (https://pmem.io/glossary/) defines ADR as flushing only the memory-controller write-pending queues, eADR as also flushing CPU caches; Intel's eADR article states eADR is new with 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable (Ice Lake) and Optane PMem 200 (https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/develop/articles/eadr-new-opportunities-for-persistent-memory-applications.html; https://www.servethehome.com/intel-optane-persistent-memory-200-series-sees-more-disclosures/). R740xd is Skylake/Cascade Lake only, and eADR is an Optane-platform feature anyway; no NVDIMM-N platform offers it. DDIO: inbound NIC writes land in LLC, outside the ADR domain; documented fixes are receiver-side CLWB/CLFLUSHOPT+SFENCE after completion, or disabling DDIO on the NIC's root port (SNIA/Intel RDMA-with-PM deck https://www.snia.org/sites/default/files/SDC15_presentations/persistant_mem/ChetDouglas_RDMA_with_PM.pdf; US11709774). Dell: R740/R740xd reference guide lists NVDIMM-N as 6 per CPU, 12 per system, 16 GB each (192 GB), slot 1 of each channel (https://downloads.dell.com/manuals/common/poweredgeR740-R740xd_RG_en-us.pdf); NVDIMM-N User Guide confirms 16 GB modules and the save-to-flash on AC loss (https://dl.dell.com/topicspdf/nvdimm_n_user_guide_en-us.pdf). Caveats, not errors: one chassis battery is mandatory and in the R740xd it occupies a 3.5-inch mid-drive-tray bay (2.5-inch mid tray precludes NVDIMM-N); Dell resellers list two CPUs plus 12 RDIMMs as the minimum config; Dell sold no 32 GB NVDIMM-N and the part is end-of-life (used market only). Rocky 9 (5.14) and Rocky 10 (6.12) handle NVDIMM-N identically via NFIT/libnvdimm pmem+DAX; 6.12's IBTA RDMA FLUSH verb exists only in soft-RoCE, not mlx5, and cannot help on Skylake.
- Design impact: The Durable ack path must include a receiver-side CLWB+SFENCE over the landed bytes (software-notified GPSPM style) because no NIC-side verb or BIOS DDIO knob on Dell 14G can make inbound writes persistent; NVLOG ring capacity is capped at 192 GB raw, in practice two 96 GB per-socket interleave sets, and the hardware is obsolete, so the design should treat NVDIMM-N as optional and keep a battery-backed-RAM-plus-mirror fallback.

### [NETWORK-8] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Network)
- Claim: A single 10GbE link (1,250 MB/s payload) is below the sequential flush capability of a 24-drive SAS RAID6 array (roughly 2.6-3.3 GB/s raw, 1-1.5 GB/s through md's single raid5d thread), so the mirror link, not the disks, is the pair's write ceiling unless 2 x 10GbE, 25GbE or 16/32GFC is used.
- Correction: With default md tuning and a 10GbE mirror link, the two ceilings are roughly equal, not clearly separated: a single 10GbE carries ~1,180 MB/s TCP payload at 1500 MTU (~1,235 MB/s with 9000 MTU, ~1,100-1,200 MB/s for a real iSCSI/TCP session), not 1,250 MB/s. The "1-1.5 GB/s through one raid5d thread" is a default-configuration artifact, not a hardware limit: group_thread_cnt (kernel 3.12+, present in both Rocky 9's 5.14 and Rocky 10's 6.12) and a larger stripe_cache_size move stripe handling off the single thread (Shaohua Li's commit shows 600 to 1,150 MB/s on an Atom with 9 HDDs; a RAID6 resync report shows 1.3 to 10.5 GB/s at group_thread_cnt=12), and splitting 24 drives into two 12-drive RAID6 arrays gives two raid5d threads. Also, the claim compares the wrong pair: ingest cannot exceed the front-end link either, so the pair's write ceiling is min(front-end link, mirror link, tuned flush rate), and all three are ~1.2-2+ GB/s today. Kernel caveat: 6.0-6.3.1 had a raid5 sequential-write regression ("Pivot raid5_make_request"), fixed in 6.3.2 and thus present-fixed in 6.12; 5.14 is unaffected.
- Evidence: Shaohua Li md/raid5 multithread commit (raid5d bottleneck, group_thread_cnt numbers): https://lore.kernel.org/all/87zifvj61v.fsf@esperi.org.uk/T/ ; Nix report of md_raid5 CPU saturation with group_thread_cnt=0: https://lore.kernel.org/all/874lxvgt4s.fsf@esperi.org.uk/ ; Launchpad RAID6 group_thread_cnt scaling (1.3 to 10.5 GB/s, plateau at 12): https://bugs.launchpad.net/ubuntu/+source/mdadm/+bug/1940207 ; Jan Kara 6.3.2 "md/raid5: Improve performance for sequential IO": https://code.opensuse.org/kernel/kernel-source/blob/0846cdf553aa509a04d56c3dca2dc822abaefb98/f/patches.kernel.org/6.3.2-113-md-raid5-Improve-performance-for-sequential-IO.patch ; QNAP single-port 10GbE iSCSI ~1,130-1,180 MB/s: https://www.qnap.com/en/performance/model/ts-464 . Arithmetic: 10 Gb/s = 1,250 MB/s line rate minus ~5% L2/IP/TCP overhead at 1500 MTU; 22 data drives x 120-250 MB/s (2.5" 10k SAS, inner-to-outer) = 2.6-5.5 GB/s raw, so the raw disk figure is fine and the HBA330/backplane (8 x 12Gb lanes, PCIe 3.0 x8 ~7.9 GB/s) is not binding.
- Design impact: Do not size NVLOG from the mirror link alone; size it from min(front-end ingest, mirror link, measured CP flush rate) and treat the md flush rate as a tunable you must set (group_thread_cnt, stripe_cache_size, possibly two arrays) and benchmark on the real R740xd. The link-upgrade recommendation (2x10GbE needs multiple TCP flows for LACP to help; 25GbE ~3 GB/s; 16GFC ~1.6 GB/s; 32GFC ~3.2 GB/s) stands, but once the link exceeds ~1.5 GB/s the untuned md array becomes the ceiling and NVLOG fills, so CP back-pressure design is needed regardless.

### [STORAGE-1] CONFIRMED (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: On RHEL/Rocky 9.4 and later the sysctl kernel.io_uring_disabled defaults to 2, so io_uring_setup() returns EPERM for all processes until it is set to 0.
- Evidence: RHEL 9.4 release notes (kernel parameter changes) list io_uring_disabled as an updated sysctl with "2 (default): io_uring creation is disabled for all processes. io_uring_setup() always fails with -EPERM. Existing instances keep working" plus new io_uring_group (default -1 = CAP_SYS_ADMIN only): https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/Red_Hat_Enterprise_Linux/9/html/9.4_release_notes/kernel_parameters_changes. The 9.4 and 9.5 Technology Preview chapters (Jira RHEL-11792) repeat "By default, this feature is disabled ... 2 ... This is the default setting" and add that an updated SELinux policy (mmap on anonymous inodes) is also required: https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/9/html/9.4_release_notes/technology-previews. RHEL 10.0 release notes (sections 4.4/4.5) carry the same default of 2 on the 6.12 kernel, and Red Hat Jira RHEL-65347 says io_uring is Tech Preview and disabled by default in RHEL 10: https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/10/html-single/10.0_release_notes/index. Upstream (6.6+) defaults to 0, so this is a Red Hat downstream choice (Jeff Moyer's "RHEL: disable io_uring support" patch, reassessed per minor release). Caveats that do not change the verdict: (a) before kernel 5.14.0-362.8.1.el9_3 io_uring was not available at all (ENOSYS), so "9.4 and later" is where it first becomes switchable; (b) value 1 with CAP_SYS_ADMIN or io_uring_group membership is an alternative to 0; (c) the sysctl is runtime-writable by root with no one-way lock (unlike kexec_load_disabled); (d) io_uring remains Technology Preview through 9.8/10.0 (unsupported by Red Hat); (e) container seccomp profiles return ENOSYS independently. Independent corroboration: PostgreSQL test_aio patch (Dec 2025) and Rocky forum thread.
- Design impact: The D-Blade io_uring path is viable but only after OpenTAP's installer ships a /etc/sysctl.d drop-in setting kernel.io_uring_disabled=1 (run the daemon with CAP_SYS_ADMIN or in io_uring_group) or 0, plus the required SELinux policy update; since OpenTAP is the appliance OS it controls this, so the pwritev2 fallback is only needed for hardened/third-party hosts. Note io_uring is Technology Preview (not Red Hat-supported) on both Rocky 9 (5.14) and Rocky 10 (6.12), so a startup probe that detects EPERM/ENOSYS and fails loudly or degrades should still exist.

### [STORAGE-2] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: In block/fops.c, dio_bio_write_op() sets REQ_FUA on a direct-I/O write when the iocb is DSYNC, and md raid456 forwards FUA/PREFLUSH to member devices, so O_DIRECT|O_DSYNC writes to /dev/mdX are FUA writes.
- Correction: True on the code path, with caveats. (1) 6.12 block/fops.c dio_bio_write_op(): `if (iocb_is_dsync(iocb)) opf |= REQ_FUA`; 5.14 fs/block_dev.c identical via `iocb->ki_flags & IOCB_DSYNC`. (2) raid456 forwards FUA: ops_run_biodrain sets R5_WantFUA, ops_complete_reconstruct propagates it to all written + parity devs, ops_run_io issues member bios with REQ_FUA. (3) But PREFLUSH is not "forwarded" as a flag: md_flush_request() fans out separate empty REQ_PREFLUSH bios to every active member, then clears PREFLUSH on the data bio. (4) blkdev_write_iter() still calls generic_write_sync() after the FUA write, so every O_DIRECT|O_DSYNC write = FUA member writes + a trailing blkdev_issue_flush fanned to N disks. (5) md_end_flush ignores member flush bi_status (6.12 comment: "bi_status of bio does not affect the bi_status of parent"; 5.14 never checks it) — a successful flush on /dev/mdX does not prove every member flushed. 5.14 also coalesces concurrent flushes (prev_flush_start). (6) At each member, blk_insert_flush drops FUA/PREFLUSH if the queue reports no write cache (PERC virtual disks with controller write-back cache, or drives with WCE off) and converts FUA to a post-flush if the queue lacks BLK_FEAT_FUA. Platter durability therefore depends on the member exposing a volatile cache honestly (HBA330/mpt3sas SAS with DPOFUA: yes; PERC VD: controller BBU, not platters). (7) raid5 write-back journal (r5c) takes a different path (log_handle_flush_request); not verified here.
- Evidence: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/block/fops.c (dio_bio_write_op, blkdev_write_iter→generic_write_sync, blkdev_fsync→blkdev_issue_flush); https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v5.14/fs/block_dev.c (same); https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/include/linux/fs.h (generic_write_sync unconditional on dsync); https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/drivers/md/raid5.c (R5_WantFUA, ops_run_io, raid5_make_request PREFLUSH); https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/drivers/md/md.c and v5.14 (md_flush_request, md_end_flush, md_init_stacking_limits sets BLK_FEAT_WRITE_CACHE|BLK_FEAT_FUA); https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v6.12/block/blk-flush.c (blk_insert_flush policy).
- Design impact: The CP ordering is reachable, but each O_DSYNC write costs FUA + N member flushes; prefer plain O_DIRECT data writes, then one explicit fdatasync/flush, then FUA-superblock (via O_DSYNC) rather than O_DSYNC on every data write. Do not treat a successful md flush as proof of member durability; md swallows member flush errors, so monitor member error state independently. Verify member queues expose write_cache=write back and FUA (check /sys/block/sdX/queue/fua, write_cache); on PERC VDs durability rests on the BBU, not FUA.

### [STORAGE-3] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: md PPL works only for RAID5 (not RAID6), is limited to 64 member devices, and costs 30-40% of write throughput; the md journal is the only md-native write-hole closure for RAID6.
- Correction: True in substance, with two precision fixes. (1) PPL is RAID5-only: raid5-ppl.c ppl_init_log() returns -EINVAL with "PPL is not compatible with raid level %d" when mddev->level != 5; mdadm man: "ppl: For RAID5 only". (2) The disk cap is sizeof(unsigned long)*8 (disk_flush_bitmap), i.e. 64 on x86_64 (32 on 32-bit); no RAID5 build would hit it anyway. (3) The throughput cost is "reduced by UP TO 30-40%, scales with the number of drives" from Intel's 2017 design doc/patch cover letter, not a measured constant on modern HDD arrays; treat it as an upper-bound estimate. Additional constraints the claim omits: PPL is incompatible with a write-intent bitmap and with the journal, requires PAGE_SIZE==4096, and does not protect in-flight data or stripes that lose a dirty disk. (4) For RAID6 the md journal (--write-journal; write-through since 4.4, write-back since 4.10) is indeed the only md-native write-hole closure; the write-intent bitmap only shortens resync and does not close the hole. Identical on Rocky 9 (5.14) and Rocky 10 (6.12): PPL has been in-tree since 4.12 and nothing changed by 6.12.
- Evidence: Kernel PPL doc (https://docs.kernel.org/driver-api/md/raid5-ppl.html): "feature available for RAID5 arrays", "maximum 64 disks", "write performance is reduced by up to 30%-40% but it scales with the number of drives", "write-intent bitmap and PPL together is not supported", "not a true journal... does not protect from losing in-flight data". Source (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/drivers/md/raid5-ppl.c): level != 5 check, max_disks = sizeof_field(disk_flush_bitmap)*BITS_PER_BYTE, bitmap and journal incompatibility checks, PAGE_SIZE != 4096 check. mdadm(8) (https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/mdadm.8.html): "ppl: For RAID5 only"; "journal: For RAID levels 4/5/6". Journal doc (https://www.kernel.org/doc/html/latest/driver-api/md/raid5-cache.html): raid4/5/6, write-through 4.4, write-back 4.10, cache-disk failure in write-back = data loss. LWN 2017 PPL series (https://lwn.net/Articles/715280) has the same 30-40% wording, confirming its origin as a design estimate.
- Design impact: The justification stands: on a RAID6 aggregate md offers no PPL, so the only md-native option is a journal SSD, which is a single point of failure in write-back mode and adds a serialising write path. OpenTAP's own closure (never-overwrite CP flush, full-stripe-replicated superblock) remains the right call, but the design should also decide whether to keep a write-intent bitmap (fine with the journal, impossible with PPL) for fast post-crash resync, since OpenTAP's log structure does not remove md's own stripe-consistency resync obligation.

### [STORAGE-4] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: A 7200 rpm 3.5in NL-SAS/SATA drive sustains roughly 200-250 MB/s sequential on outer tracks and 100-130 MB/s on inner tracks, and delivers about 80 random 4 KiB IOPS at QD1 and 150-200 at QD32.
- Correction: Random IOPS are right: ~75-85 at QD1 (4.16 ms rotational + ~8-9 ms seek ≈ 12.5 ms), and 150-210 at QD16-32 (Seagate specs 170 read IOPS at 4K QD16 for Exos X16/X18, 168 for X24; StorageReview measured 205 IOPS at QD32 on Exos M 30TB). Sequential figures are one generation stale: current 16-24 TB 7200 rpm CMR drives (Exos X18/X20/X24, Ultrastar HC550/HC560, Toshiba MG09/MG10) are rated 261-285 MB/s OD and measure 272-277 MB/s; inner tracks run roughly half OD, so ~130-145 MB/s. The 200-250 / 100-130 range applies to 4-10 TB class drives. Figures assume CMR (never SMR), large sequential I/O with NCQ, and no parity read-modify-write.
- Evidence: Seagate Exos X18 datasheet: 270 MB/s OD (18 TB), 261 MB/s (16 TB), 170/550 IOPS 4K QD16, 4.16 ms latency (https://www.seagate.com/files/www-content/datasheets/pdfs/exos-x18-channel-DS2045-2-2010US-en_CA.pdf). KitGuru HC560 20 TB: 277 MB/s peak read, 272 MB/s CDM (https://www.kitguru.net/components/hard-drives/simon-crisp/wd-ultrastar-dc-hc560-20tb-hdd-review/all/1/). Toshiba MG09/MG10: 281 MB/s sustained (https://www.ithome.com.tw/review/155685). StorageReview Exos M 30TB: 205 IOPS 4K random read 16T/QD32, Exos X16 spec 170/440 IOPS (https://www.storagereview.com/review/seagate-exos-m-30tb-review-hamr-ing-storage-density, https://www.storagereview.com/review/seagate-exos-x16-16tb-hdd-review). PCPer WD 6TB: random performance roughly doubles QD1 to QD32 (https://pcper.com/2015/09/western-digital-6tb-black-and-6tb-red-pro-review-bigger-and-faster/4/). Hardwareluxx: inner-track ≈ half OD (https://www.hardwareluxx.de/community/threads/seagate-exos-2x18-mit-18-oder-16-tb.1327385/). Arithmetic: 12 × ~120 MB/s ≈ 1.4 GB/s is the inner-track worst case; with current drives the band is 1.6-3.3 GB/s raw before parity overhead. Kernel version (5.14 vs 6.12) is irrelevant to these physical limits.
- Design impact: The 1.4 GB/s absorb figure is a conservative worst case (inner tracks, older drives) and stays valid; with 16+ TB drives it is 15-30% pessimistic, so CP duration and spindle-count margins are slightly better than estimated, but the read-IOPS ceiling (1-2.4K for 12 drives) and therefore the SSD-tier recommendation stand unchanged. Spec-sheet rates exclude RAID parity, RMW, and mixed read/write seek interference, so the 12-drive mdadm array should be modeled at 60-80% of raw sequential and verified with fio on the actual array before fixing CP timing.

### [STORAGE-5] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: The Dell R740xd supports at most 12 NVDIMM-N modules of 16 GB (192 GB) in dual-CPU configurations with Gold/Platinum-class Xeon Scalable CPUs, and the NVDIMM battery kit excludes the 2.5in mid-drive tray.
- Correction: Dell documents for the R740xd: max 12x 16 GB NVDIMM-N = 192 GB; only 1/2/4/6/12-module configs; dual processors mandatory ("Not supported with single processor") and a minimum of 12 RDIMMs (A1-A6, B1-B6) must be present, so the 12-NVDIMM config caps DRAM at 12 RDIMM slots; LRDIMMs and 8 GB RDIMMs cannot be mixed with NVDIMM-N. The Gold/Platinum-only restriction is a VMware ESXi support condition (Gold 51xx/61xx, Platinum 81xx), not a Dell hardware/BIOS requirement for Linux; Dell's RHEL guidance has no SKU tier. The VMware HCL lists the R740xd only with 1st-gen (Skylake-SP 8100/6100/5100) CPUs, while R740/R640 add 2nd-gen 8200/6200/5200, so Cascade Lake + NVDIMM-N on the xd is unverified. The 2.5-inch mid-tray exclusion for the battery appears only in reseller/ITCreations copy (battery fits air shroud, 3.5-inch mid-tray bracket, or a bracket, and consumes one mid-tray slot); Dell's ISM topic exists but its text could not be retrieved.
- Evidence: Dell EMC NVDIMM-N Persistent Memory User Guide (dl.dell.com/topicspdf/nvdimm_n_user_guide_en-us.pdf, mirror pcserverandparts.com): "maximum of 12x 16GB NVDIMM-Ns ... 192GB", Table 2 population (A7-A12, B7-B12 with RDIMMs A1-A6, B1-B6), ESXi-only Gold/Platinum clause, BIOS 1.3.7 / iDRAC 3.15.15.15 / FW 9324 minimums. R740/R740xd Technical Guide (i.dell.com ... poweredge-r740-r740xd-technical-guide-190712.pdf): "Supported configurations have dual processors and a minimum of 12x RDIMMs"; "Maximum of 12 NVDIMM-Ns"; figures show battery on air shroud or on the hard-drive tray. Dell R740xd Technical Specifications (dell.com/support/manuals/.../memory-specifications): "Not supported with single processor"; 8 GB RDIMMs not mixable. VMware KB 54444 (knowledge.broadcom.com/external/article?legacyId=54444): R740XD row lists only 8100/6100/5100, 192 GB certified. 2.5-inch mid-tray exclusion: blog.itcreations.com/dell-emc-poweredge-r740xd-server-review and expresscomputersystems.com configurators (secondary). Arithmetic: 12 x 16 GB = 192 GB holds.
- Design impact: 192 GB PMem is the hard ceiling and it costs you half the DIMM slots: with 12 NVDIMM-Ns the RAM write cache lives in only 12 RDIMMs, so size DRAM (and the 2x10GbE mirror window) against that, and treat NVDIMM-N (EOL, 2666 MT/s SR, used-market parts) as an optional tier rather than the baseline. Do not plan on Silver CPUs being blocked on Linux, but validate Cascade Lake + NVDIMM-N on the xd before buying; order 3.5-inch mid trays (or none) if PMem is wanted.

### [STORAGE-6] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: Dell MD1400/MD1420 shelves with dual EMMs support SAS/NL-SAS drives (SATA is not listed) in redundant-path mode and require an external 12 Gb SAS HBA or PERC H830/H840, not the internal-only HBA330.
- Correction: Dell's MD14xx support matrix lists SAS, NL-SAS and SAS SSD only (no SATA) and supports exactly four host adapters: HBA355e (current, 14G-16G), the older Dell 12 Gbps SAS HBA, PERC H840 and PERC H830. The HBA330 (internal-only ports) is not among them, so the claim's HBA list is right but omits the HBA355e, which is the part you would actually buy for an R740xd today. Two important corrections: (1) Dell's "redundant path" mode means ONE server cabling both EMMs to the same controller (HBA355e ports 0+1 or 2+3), not two servers; the support matrix states "Maximum number of servers: 1" and that drives "cannot be accessed in split mode between two" hosts. A two-node shared-shelf topology (each node's HBA to one EMM) works physically with dual-ported SAS drives and is reported in the field, but it is outside Dell's support matrix. (2) PERC H830/H840 are RAID controllers that own the disks ("only the server that has the PERC installed can see the storage"); for mdadm on shared shelves only a non-RAID HBA (HBA355e / 12Gb SAS HBA) is viable.
- Evidence: Dell MD14xx Enclosure Support Matrix, supported adapters page: https://www.dell.com/support/manuals/en-us/storage-md1400/md14xx_sm_pub/supported-adapters-and-controllers (HBA355e, 12Gbps SAS HBA, H840, H830). Rev A07/A09 PDFs (https://dl.dell.com/topicspdf/md1400md1420_sm_en-us.pdf) state "can only be attached to a single server", max servers 1. Dell support thread: https://www.dell.com/community/en/conversations/powervault/powervault-md1400/647f8be0f4ccf8a8dec0b6ed ("single server only", no split mode, PERC H840 => only owning server sees disks, use a non-RAID SAS HBA). HBA355e dual-path port table: https://www.dell.com/support/manuals/en-us/poweredge-r7525/hba355_ug/port-support-matrix-for-dual-path-on-the-md1400-and-md1420-enclosures. Dell datasheet lists NL-SAS/SAS/SSD only. Proxmox report of two hosts each seeing all MD1420 disks (unsupported): https://forum.proxmox.com/goto/post?id=551125. Kernel version is irrelevant here (mpt3sas supports HBA355e in both 5.14 and 6.12).
- Design impact: The shared-shelf HA model still needs SAS/NL-SAS drives and one external HBA per node, but it must be an HBA355e (or 12Gb SAS HBA), never PERC H830/H840, and OpenTAP must document that dual-host attachment of MD14xx is unsupported by Dell and fenced/validated by OpenTAP itself. Alternatively, standardize on a shelf that formally supports two hosts (e.g. ME4/ME5 or third-party dual-host JBODs) if vendor support matters; SATA drives force the shadow-replica model regardless.

### [STORAGE-7] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: Cascade Lake platforms provide ADR but not eADR, so CPU caches are outside the persistence domain and each NVLOG record must be written back with CLWB/CLFLUSHOPT plus SFENCE before it is durable; stable Rust exposes no _mm_clwb intrinsic, so this must be emitted with asm!.
- Correction: Hardware part is right: eADR arrived with 3rd-gen Xeon (Ice Lake) + Optane 200; Cascade Lake/R740xd (Optane DCPMM 100, App Direct, 2nd-gen Xeon only) has ADR, which covers memory-controller write queues but not CPU caches, so cached stores need an explicit writeback plus SFENCE. The software part overstates: (1) _mm_clwb is indeed absent from stable Rust (stable doc page 404) and _mm_clflushopt is nightly-only (feature simd_x86_clflushopt, tracking rust-lang/rust#157096, added mid-2026), but asm! is NOT the only route: _mm_clflush (stable since 1.27, slower, invalidates the line) works, and non-temporal stores (_mm_stream_si64/_mm_stream_si128, stable 1.27) + _mm_sfence bypass the cache entirely and are durable on ADR with no flush instruction (what PMDK libpmem does for large copies). asm! (stable since 1.59) emitting `clwb` is a legitimate choice, not a necessity. (2) The quoted commit sequence "payload, fence, header, writeback, fence" is wrong as written: SFENCE alone does not make the payload durable; the payload must be CLWB'd (or NT-stored) before the first SFENCE, then header stored, CLWB'd, SFENCE'd.
- Evidence: pmem.io glossary (ADR flushes memory-controller queues; eADR also flushes CPU caches): https://pmem.io/glossary/ ; Intel eADR article describes eADR as new in 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable (Ice Lake); Cascade Lake was the first Optane platform: https://www.nextplatform.com/2020/12/18/being-persistent-with-persistent-memory . R740xd/2nd-gen Xeon + Optane App Direct: https://aws.principledtechnologies.com/Dell/PowerEdge-R740xd-Intel-Optane-1019.pdf . Rust: _mm_clflushopt nightly-only (https://doc.rust-lang.org/stable/core/arch/x86_64/fn._mm_clflushopt.html, tracking https://github.com/rust-lang/rust/issues/157096, stdarch#1456 closed 2026-06-01); _mm_clwb stable page returns 404; _mm_stream_si64 stable 1.27 with SFENCE requirement: https://doc.rust-lang.org/stable/core/arch/x86_64/fn._mm_stream_si64.html . Kernel version (Rocky 9 5.14 vs Rocky 10 6.12) is irrelevant: durability of DAX-mapped App Direct stores is purely CPU/platform semantics.
- Design impact: Keep the ADR assumption and explicit flush+fence discipline, but fix the commit sequence (writeback payload before the first SFENCE) and choose the mechanism deliberately: NT stores + SFENCE for the bulk payload (stable intrinsics, no cache pollution) and a small asm! `clwb` (or stable _mm_clflush) for the 8-byte header; gate on CPUID CLWB at startup and have a tested fallback. Also plan for the Optane end-of-life/supply risk, since the NVLOG design is tied to DCPMM 100-series hardware.

### [STORAGE-8] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Storage)
- Claim: 2x10GbE carries about 2.2 GB/s of block payload, i.e. 22 GB per 10 s CP, while a 12-drive RAID6 of 7.2K HDDs absorbs about 1.4 GB/s before GC and 0.7-0.9 GB/s after, so sustained line-rate writes overflow a 64 GB write-cache budget in roughly 45-60 s.
- Correction: Directionally right (one 12-HDD RAID6 cannot sustain 2x10GbE line-rate writes), but three details are off. (1) The 45-60 s figure only holds for the post-GC drain: 64 GB / (2.2 - 0.7..0.9 GB/s) = 43-49 s; with the quoted pre-GC 1.4 GB/s drain it is ~80 s. (2) 1.4 GB/s is pessimistic for current drives: 16-20 TB Exos/Ultrastar 7.2K spec 270-291 MB/s OD (~210 MB/s platter average), so 10 data spindles doing full-stripe log writes can reach ~2.0-2.1 GB/s average and ~2.7 GB/s on outer tracks; 1.3-1.4 GB/s is roughly md's single raid5d-thread ceiling, which group_thread_cnt (Rocky 9 and 10 both support it; 6.12 scales it better) lifts. With new drives the gap to 2.2 GB/s ingest nearly closes pre-GC. (3) The scenario presumes mirror bandwidth >= ingest. With the stated 1/10GbE partner link, acknowledged write ingest is capped at ~0.11 GB/s (1GbE) or ~1.1 GB/s (one 10GbE), both at or below HDD drain, so the cache cannot overflow at all; if front-end and mirror share the same 2x10GbE, ingest halves to ~1.1-1.25 GB/s. Overflow only becomes real with a dedicated >=20 Gb/s interconnect (2x10GbE, FC, RDMA).
- Evidence: Arithmetic: 20 Gb/s = 2.5 GB/s raw, ~2.2-2.3 GB/s iSCSI payload with jumbo frames; 22 GB/10 s checks. HDD spec: Exos X20 285 MB/s max sustained OD (https://www.storagereview.com/review/seagate-exos-x20-20tb-enterprise-hdd-review); WD HC560 ~291 MB/s. md RAID6 thread ceiling ~1.3 GB/s without group_thread_cnt, ~10 GB/s resync with it (https://bugs.launchpad.net/ubuntu/+source/mdadm/+bug/1940207); 2026 md/raid5 scalability series auto-sizes group_thread_cnt (https://lkml.iu.edu/2607.1/06705.html). Overflow times computed from 64 GB / (ingest - drain).
- Design impact: The re-scoping to burst absorption is still justified, but the binding constraint in the near-term 1/10GbE design is the mirror link, not spindle count: backpressure must be sized to interconnect bandwidth first, and HDD-drain backpressure only matters once FC/RDMA mirroring lands. Budget the drain at ~2 GB/s pre-GC with tuned md (group_thread_cnt, stripe_cache_size) and modern drives, and treat the GC write-amplification factor (not raw spindle rate) as the number that decides shelf count.

### [CONSENSUS-1] CONFIRMED (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Consensus)
- Claim: openraft 0.9.25 is the latest stable release (0.10 is alpha), supports only joint-consensus membership change, exposes ensure_linearizable()/get_read_log_id() for linearizable reads, provides an AsyncRuntime trait with a TokioRuntime implementation, and its followers ignore VoteRequests while their leader lease is valid.
- Evidence: crates.io API (https://crates.io/api/v1/crates/openraft, fetched 2026-10-12): max_stable_version 0.9.25 (published 2026-07-28); newest 0.10.0-alpha.37 (2026-10-10), so 0.10 is still alpha. docs.rs 0.9.25 Raft struct (https://docs.rs/openraft/0.9.25/openraft/raft/struct.Raft.html): ensure_linearizable() and get_read_log_id() present and non-deprecated (is_leader deprecated since 0.9.0); change_membership commits a joint config then a uniform config. Dynamic-membership doc (https://docs.rs/openraft/0.9.25/openraft/docs/cluster_control/dynamic_membership/index.html): "considers all memberships as joint"; a uniform config is the single-config special case, so there is no single-step path. Crate root re-exports openraft::async_runtime::AsyncRuntime (trait) and TokioRuntime (struct). Source release-0.9 engine_impl.rs handle_vote_req (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/databendlabs/openraft/release-0.9/openraft/src/engine/engine_impl.rs): if the local vote is committed and now <= vote_last_modified + lease, the request is rejected with VoteResponse{vote_granted:false}. Two wording nuances: followers reject (reply false), they do not silently ignore; and the check only applies when the local vote is committed (node has seen an established leader) — a node that never saw a leader grants normally. No pre-vote phase exists; the lease is the substitute.
- Design impact: Library choice stands: pin openraft 0.9.25 (not 0.10 alphas, whose API is still shifting). The no-flip-flop argument holds only via the lease: a healed partitioned node with a higher term gets rejected by lease-protected followers, but since replies are explicit rejections, OpenTAP's simulation should model lease expiry (election_timeout-derived) and clock skew, as a lease on a paused/slow follower is the remaining disruption window.

### [CONSENSUS-2] CONFIRMED (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Consensus)
- Claim: tikv/raft-rs is a sans-I/O core whose crates.io release is 0.7.0 (2023) while its master branch received commits in March-May 2026, and it supports ConfChangeV2 joint consensus, ReadIndex and lease-based reads, pre-vote and check-quorum.
- Evidence: crates.io API (https://crates.io/api/v1/crates/raft): max/newest version 0.7.0, published 2023-03-07; nothing since. GitHub master commit log (https://github.com/tikv/raft-rs/commits/master): commits on 2026-02-26, 03-02 (remove protobuf dep under prost-codec, #579), 03-18 (#574), 04-14 (bump rand 0.9.3, #586), 05-13 (shrink unstable entries buffer, #589) — matches "March-May 2026" and is a low-volume maintenance cadence (~5 commits in 2026, ~2-3 in 2025). Feature set verified in source: src/config.rs on master has pre_vote, check_quorum, read_only_option (Safe default / LeaseBased, which requires check_quorum=true); src/read_only.rs implements ReadIndex via heartbeat-context acks and LeaseBased with a clock-drift warning; docs.rs 0.7.0 documents ConfChangeV2 joint consensus via propose_conf_change and recommends EntryConfChangeV2. README states the crate "includes the core Consensus Module only" — user supplies Log/Storage, state machine, and Transport and drives the Ready loop, i.e. sans-I/O in substance though the project never uses that term. Caveats that don't overturn the claim: (1) the 2026 master changes (prost-codec without protobuf, rand 0.9.3, memory-shrink fix) are unreleased; crates.io 0.7.0 still pulls rust-protobuf 2.x and old deps, so a fallback would likely need a git dependency or vendored fork; (2) Cargo.toml on master still says version 0.7.0, so no 0.8 is imminent; (3) maintenance is TiKV-driven and thin — a handful of commits/year, no published roadmap.
- Design impact: raft-rs remains a viable fallback to openraft, but plan on pinning a git rev (or vendoring) rather than the 2023 crates.io release, and budget for writing the storage, transport, and async driver layers yourself since the crate deliberately provides none; LeaseBased reads require check_quorum and are clock-drift sensitive, so prefer Safe/ReadIndex for the HA pair's metadata reads.

### [CONSENSUS-3] CONFIRMED (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Consensus)
- Claim: Red Hat removed qla2xxx target mode (the tcm_qla2xxx module) from RHEL 8 kernels and targetcli (Bugzilla 1666377, 'Management decided to disable qla2xxx target support in RHEL8'), and RHEL 9/10 and Rocky inherit that removal.
- Evidence: Bugzilla 1666377 ("Remove qla2xxx target support", RHEL 8, python-rtslib, CLOSED ERRATA RHEA-2020:1892): Red Hat maintainer states "Management decided to disable qla2xxx target support in RHEL8", "we just removed the tcm_qla2xxx kernel module (the target implementation)", initiator mode stays; release note: "Target mode is disabled for the qla2xxx QLogic Fibre Channel driver in RHEL 8.0". https://bugzilla.redhat.com/show_bug.cgi?id=1666377

RHEL 9/10 inheritance is not inferred, it is in the sources: CentOS Stream 9 (5.14) and CentOS Stream 10 (6.12) kernel configs both carry "# CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX is not set" (https://gitlab.com/redhat/centos-stream/src/kernel/centos-stream-9/-/raw/main/redhat/configs/rhel/generic/CONFIG_TCM_QLA2XXX and the same path under centos-stream-10). python-rtslib in c9s and c10s (2.1.76) still applies 0001-Turn-off-unsupported-fabrics.patch, which comments out "qla2xxx": Qla2xxxFabricModule (plus efct, tcm_fc, sbp, vhost); c10s changelog 2024-10-30 "RHEL-64990 ... disable unsupported fabrics". Rocky rebuilds these SRPMs unmodified, so Rocky 9 and 10 ship no tcm_qla2xxx.ko and a targetcli that cannot create a qla2xxx fabric. No ELRepo kmod for tcm_qla2xxx was found.

Caveat (does not weaken the claim): qla_target.o is compiled unconditionally into qla2xxx.ko (upstream Makefile, v6.12), so the in-driver target-mode core and its exported qlt_* symbols are present in the stock Rocky driver; only the LIO fabric glue module is missing.
- Design impact: An FC mirror link on stock Rocky 9/10 with QLogic HBAs cannot use LIO/targetcli target mode; OpenTAP must either build tcm_qla2xxx out of tree against the Rocky kernel (feasible since qla2xxx exports the target hooks, but unsupported and must track every kernel update), use SCST's qla2x00t, run a custom kernel, or switch the FC link to a non-LIO design. The same applies to the FC front end if the roadmap expected to expose LUNs over FC; Emulex (efct) is disabled in rtslib too, so changing HBA vendor does not help.

### [CONSENSUS-4] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Consensus)
- Claim: A SCSI-3 PERSISTENT RESERVE OUT PREEMPT AND ABORT issued by the survivor causes the preempted node's subsequent writes to the same SAS drive to fail with RESERVATION CONFLICT, enforced by the drive firmware, and this works through SAS expanders on dual-EMM JBODs with dual-ported SAS/NL-SAS drives, whereas SATA drives are single-ported and cannot be shared by two hosts without interposers.
- Correction: Mechanism is right but incomplete. (1) PREEMPT AND ABORT only fences if a reservation of type Write-Exclusive/Exclusive-Access Registrants-Only (type 5/6, or 1/3) is held or established by the survivor; a preempt with no reservation in place merely drops the victim's registration and its writes still succeed. (2) PR is per I_T nexus but the reservation scopes the whole LU across both drive ports; preempting a key removes every I_T nexus registered with that key, so the dual-EMM/expander path is fine, but the survivor must itself register on every path it will use (mpathpersist/all paths), or its own secondary path gets RESERVATION CONFLICT. (3) Fence is per drive, not per array: mdadm has no PR integration, so fencing N members is N non-atomic commands; the preempted node's md marks members Faulty on -EBADE, which is the desired effect but is not instantaneous for I/O already acked into the victim's RAM cache. (4) Linux: IOC_PR_PREEMPT_ABORT/sg_persist work on both Rocky 9 (5.14) and Rocky 10 (6.12); 6.5+ renamed BLK_STS_NEXUS to BLK_STS_RESV_CONFLICT (still -EBADE) and 6.4+ improved dm-multipath pr_ops. (5) Dell's MD1400/MD1420 deployment guide states clustering (two hosts sharing one enclosure) is not supported; it works in practice via two HBAs but is unsupported. (6) SATA: correct; single-ported, and behind an expander only one STP affiliation at a time; interposers exist but PR support on them is vendor-specific and generally not relied upon.
- Evidence: T10 SPC-3 drafts (https://www.t10.org/ftp/t10/document.03/03-337r6.pdf, 03-232r4): LU-scope reservations, I_T-nexus registration, PREEMPT&ABORT only valid from a registered nexus, reservations survive resets/nexus loss. Kernel rename to BLK_STS_RESV_CONFLICT, -EBADE preserved: https://lists.infradead.org/pipermail/linux-nvme/2023-March/038375.html. Dell MD1400/MD1420 deployment guide (https://dl.dell.com/topicspdf/md1400-md1420-dg_en-us.pdf) "clustering is not supported"; field reports of two hosts seeing all disks: https://forum.proxmox.com/threads/dell-md1420-sas-hba-expansion-multi-path.126213/. SATA single-port/affiliation: https://www.t10.org/ftp/t10/document.02/02-430r3.pdf, https://www.truenas.com/community/goto/post?id=165358.
- Design impact: Keep the device-enforced fence but make it explicit: both nodes register on all paths and the survivor holds a WERO reservation on every member before preempting; treat the per-drive fence as non-atomic across the array and pair it with a lease/watchdog bound anyway. The single arbiter-disk PR idea is sound and is the only truly atomic fence. Shared MD14xx JBODs are an unsupported Dell configuration and SATA is excluded entirely.

### [CONSENSUS-5] PARTIALLY-CORRECT (claimed with medium confidence by Opus-Consensus)
- Claim: The Dell R740xd supports up to 12 NVDIMM-N modules of 16 GB (192 GB) backed by an NVDIMM battery, and that battery cannot be installed together with the 2.5-inch mid-drive tray.
- Correction: Dell's NVDIMM-N User Guide and R740/R740xd Technical Guide confirm: max 12x 16 GB NVDIMM-N = 192 GB per system, dual CPU required, only 1/2/4/6/12-module configs validated, LRDIMMs cannot be mixed, and a minimum of 12 RDIMMs must be present (so a 12-NVDIMM box is limited to 12 RDIMMs = 192 GB or 384 GB DRAM). The battery is mandatory for writes: if absent or not fully charged, BIOS restores from flash but holds NVDIMM-N in read-only/write-protect mode; the save takes about a minute; the battery is not hot-swappable. Dell documents battery mounting on the air shroud or in the mid drive tray. The specific "not with the 2.5-inch mid tray" restriction is only found in reseller/reviewer sources (consistent, but not Dell primary text); those same sources say the 3.5-inch mid tray with 2.5-inch drive adapters is a supported workaround.
- Evidence: Dell NVDIMM-N Persistent Memory User Guide (https://dl.dell.com/topicspdf/nvdimm_n_user_guide_en-us.pdf, mirror at pcserverandparts.com): "maximum of 12x 16GB NVDIMM-Ns for a total max persistent memory capacity of 192GB"; "If a battery is not installed or not fully charged ... BIOS will keep the NVDIMM-N in Read-Only mode." Dell R740/R740xd Technical Guide (https://i.dell.com/sites/csdocuments/Merchandizing_Docs/ja/poweredge-r740-r740xd-technical-guide-190712.pdf): Table 9 configs 13/14 (12x NVDIMM-N with 12x 16/32 GB RDIMMs); Figures 4-5 show battery in hard drive tray and on air shroud. 2.5-inch mid-tray exclusion: ITCreations review (https://blog.itcreations.com/dell-emc-poweredge-r740xd-server-review/) and Express Computer Systems configurator (https://expresscomputersystems.com/collections/dell-poweredge-r-series/products/dell-poweredge-r740xd-rack-server-configurable). Dell ISM pages for "NVDIMM-N battery details" returned no body text, so the restriction could not be verified against Dell. No Linux kernel difference: NVDIMM-N is exposed via ACPI NFIT/libnvdimm (nd_pmem) on both 5.14 and 6.12.
- Design impact: 192 GB NVLOG capacity holds, but a 12-NVDIMM node caps DRAM at 12 RDIMMs (192/384 GB), which bounds the RAM write cache plus Rust heap; size NVLOG and cache together. The software must treat NVDIMM write-protect (battery absent/charging/failed, reported via BIOS/iDRAC) as a first-class state and fail over or degrade to write-through, since RPO=0 only holds while the node is "armed". Order the 3.5-inch mid tray (2.5-inch adapters if needed) or skip the mid tray; and note NVDIMM-N is a 2017-era 14G-only part sourced refurbished in 2026.

### [CONSENSUS-6] CONFIRMED (claimed with high confidence by Opus-Consensus)
- Claim: iDRAC9 supports Redfish POST /redfish/v1/Systems/System.Embedded.1/Actions/ComputerSystem.Reset with ResetType ForceOff and reports PowerState for confirmation, and IPMI over LAN is disabled by default on current iDRAC9 firmware (Dell DSA-2024-099).
- Evidence: 1) Redfish ForceOff + PowerState: Dell's own iDRAC-Redfish-Scripting repo (Redfish Python/GetSetPowerStateREDFISH.py) GETs /redfish/v1/Systems/System.Embedded.1 and reads data['PowerState'], enumerates Actions.#ComputerSystem.Reset ResetType@Redfish.AllowableValues, and POSTs {'ResetType': ...} to /redfish/v1/Systems/System.Embedded.1/Actions/ComputerSystem.Reset; examples use ForceOff/On/ForceRestart. The dellemc.openmanage.redfish_powerstate Ansible module (https://docs.ansible.com/ansible/latest/collections/dellemc/openmanage/redfish_powerstate_module.html) lists ForceOff among allowed reset_type values on that same URI. PowerState is a mandatory DMTF ComputerSystem property. 2) IPMI default: Dell DSA-2024-099 (https://dell.com/support/kbdoc/en-us/000226503, CVE-2024-25943, IPMI session hijack, CVSS 7.6) states verbatim "IPMI is disabled by default" and recommends disabling IPMI over LAN as mitigation; fixed firmware is 7.00.00.172+ for 14G (R740xd) and 7.10.50.00+ for 15G/16G. Caveats I could not close: no Dell release note pins which firmware made IPMI-over-LAN default-off (the advisory asserts it, not a changelog), and the default is a factory default only; used/refurb R740xd units or SCP imports frequently have iDRAC.IPMILan.Enable=Enabled, so treat it as per-unit state (verify with `racadm get iDRAC.IPMILan.Enable`). Nothing here depends on the host kernel (Rocky 9 5.14 vs Rocky 10 6.12); it is BMC-side.
- Design impact: The Redfish rung stands: POST ForceOff, then poll PowerState until "Off" before declaring the partner fenced.
