Against: every FC target path is Red Hat-unsupported (NETWORK-2), the 50-150 us outcome is predictable, and it spends HBAs and engineer-weeks in the busiest phase.
6. **Takeover hard ceiling.** Consensus 10 s p99/15 s hard, Network 15 s, Security 25 s. CONSENSUS-7: dm-multipath defaults fail I/O at ~15 s unless ports report TRANSITIONING, so 25 s is safe only with shipped host tuning.
7. **Phase 1 scope.** The brief's Phase 1 is the mirror daemon; Storage pulls CP-lite on a real md RAID6 into it, Security the configfs applier and ESXi/Windows interop, Consensus the fence library and shelf spike, Network the FC spike. Each is defensible ("the record is the transport's contract", "no end-to-end test without a front door", "epoch in the first packet or rewrite"); together they are four Phase 1s.

#### Decisions for the lead architect
Ordered by how much each constrains the rest.

1. **Disk topology.** Recommend: shared dual-ported SAS JBOD per pair for the product (HBA355e, SAS/NL-SAS only, one EMM per host, PR fence), accepting that MD14xx two-host attach is outside Dell's matrix or choosing a vendor-documented dual-host JBOD; tell the owner "SAS/SATA" becomes "SAS" and node-local disks mean manual failover. Phase 1 runs on node-local lab hardware under a written RPO=0/RTO=repair statement, with the ring consumer specified for both models (scan-at-takeover and continuous indexer). All four converge; the device-enforced fence and undivided ingest decide it.
2. **RPO=0 wording and NVLOG medium.** Write RPO=0 as "no acknowledged write lost on any single-node failure; on correlated power loss only with the NVLOG on persistent media", and make persistent media the product baseline: Optane-class PLP NVMe (P5800X-class, separate devices for own log and inbound ring per Storage; NAND endurance fails), NVDIMM-N optional (EOL, 192 GB, half the DIMM slots). Phase 1 uses DRAM `Placed` with WCE=0 as a loudly labelled prototype; do not build the WCE=1 mini-CP path.
3. **Kernel posture.** Rocky 10.1 stock kernel, no out-of-tree modules in v1; the install profile sets `io_uring_disabled`, the SELinux URING_CMD policy and masks `target.service`; Tech Preview status accepted in writing. Consequence: no FC-SCSI (`tcm_qla2xxx`) and no Windows over FC. Owning a kernel is a permanent cost nobody budgeted.
4. **FC's role.** Relabel Tier 2 as "isolation/distance option: NVMe/FC mirror-only on Emulex LPe32002/35002, two HBAs per node, Red Hat-unsupported" and make Tier 3 (25GbE RoCE) the performance tier. Fund Network's time-boxed spike only after the owner confirms FC with those facts in hand.
5. **Record and ring contract, frozen before code.** Network's revised split: dense 64 B headers in descriptor blocks plus a 4 KiB-aligned payload ring; fields `{magic, version, hdr_crc, aggregate_id, owner_epoch, nvlog_seq, cp_seq, op, vol, lba, len, req_id, ncrc}` with per-4 KiB CRCs; keep `req_id` (8 bytes, REISSUE needs it) and add the missing `version`; ops Write/Discard/WriteZeroes/Snap/Clone/CpBoundary/Status/Control/PrUpdate. Storage owns the document; all four sign.
6. **Two transports and the fence.** Ratify `MirrorRing` (put-at-slot, durable ack, `can_reject` advisory) and `ControlLink` (TCP/mTLS for Raft, heartbeats, leases); fence = lease-at-ack plus lease-gated hardware watchdog, PR with a JBOD, Redfish as accelerator over a dedicated iDRAC NIC; epoch is replay cleanup. Consensus's timer table is the only one. Owner inputs: a witness VM (else manual failover in v1) and a separate iDRAC network.
7. **Takeover unit and processes.** Aggregate; two per 24-bay node; one ring and credit budget each. Recommend one `dbladed` per aggregate beside `nbladed` and `otap-ctl`: crash isolation matches the takeover unit and the per-aggregate leases.
8. **Mirror-down policy.** Consensus's by-NVLOG-class rule; state plainly that with volatile NVLOG a mirror link failure is a write outage (BUSY after 2 s), which is Phase 1's reality.
9. **Phase 1 boundary.** Record and NVLOG backends (DRAM, devdax, NVMe), `MirrorRing` over TCP, `ControlLink` stub (lease, heartbeat, epoch page, watchdog), ublk-behind-LIO harness, in-process generator, elle-style checker; durability, shelf and FC spikes as measurements with go/no-go outputs. CP-lite, the configfs applier and full interop stay in Phases 2-3; Storage's md flush measurement runs as a spike, not code.
10. **Performance contract.** 1GbE is functional only; 10GbE with the fixed tuning profile; Network owns the table: mirror 4K QD1 p50 <=35 us, D-Blade ack p50 <=60 us, client p50 <=200 us gate (150 target), p99 <=500 us.

#### Phase 1 risks
1. **The contract is not converged** (crossed concessions on alignment, `req_id`, no version field). Retire: a one-week sign-off yielding a byte-level layout and an `otap-record` crate with proptest round-trip, replay-prefix and corruption tests, plus a `WRITE_FIXED` CP write straight from the ring proven by a zero-staging-copy counter.
2. **Fencing on the receiver-less ring is argued, not proven**: lease-at-ack, port unlink, unmeasured nvmet association teardown, slot ABA. Retire: measure `nvmet_port_del_ctrls` teardown on nvmet-tcp; SIGSTOP-zombie and partition trials under an elle-style checker with "zero acked-but-unreplayed writes" as the sole pass criterion; a turmoil case that delays a write past slot reuse to exercise the quarantine.
3. **Tech Preview foundations may not work on the installed kernel**: io_uring is off by default on 10.x, SELinux gates URING_CMD, and REISSUE/AUTO_BUF_REG on 6.12.0-130 is inferred from changelogs (SECURITY-2). Retire in week 1: the install profile, startup probes (EPERM/ENOSYS, `UBLK_U_CMD_GET_FEATURES`), and a stopgap NBD front door (verify it is in-box) so the harness is never blocked on ublk.
4. **No hardware, no numbers.** Lab lead time for the R740xd pair, NV media, NICs, HBAs, shelf, PDU and initiators. Retire: order now; build the VM matrix (memmap=, nvmet-tcp, fcloop) so everything except RTT, flush rate and power pulls lands before hardware arrives.
5. **Takeover stall versus initiator timers.** CONSENSUS-7: dm-multipath fails I/O at ~15 s unless TRANSITIONING; ESXi pauses 25-35 s without the floating portal. Retire: manual-takeover rehearsal against real ESXi, Windows MPIO and Linux initiators with TRANSITIONING, portal reinstatement and the shipped multipath.conf; the measured stall sets Consensus's timer table.
6. **Durability without a cold-start protocol or NV media.** Retire: write the dual-power-loss reconcile protocol before the durability spike; run the 50 PDU pulls on the chosen NV medium; expose DRAM mode in the API and logs.
7. **Scope and core budget.** Four Phase 1s, no summed core count, no SKU. Retire: the boundary decision above, and a per-SKU core/memory budget measured while hitting the Phase 1 targets.

### ONTAP veteran

#### Where the plan diverges from ONTAP
**1. What is mirrored.** ONTAP mirrors the NVLOG: a journal of logged operations (request plus payload) DMA'd into the partner's "partner half" of NVRAM. The partner never parses it in steady state; it replays only on takeover. The write cache is the DRAM buffer cache, bounded by and rebuildable from the log. The brief's "RAM write cache mirrored into partner RAM" was a misunderstanding; the specialists corrected it to NVLOG-as-truth. Their standby indexer is a real departure (ONTAP replays at takeover) but a legitimate one, aimed at replay time.

**2. NVRAM is the durability mechanism.** An ack means "in two persistent logs". A DRAM log fails even the single-failure definition: HA pairs sit *in takeover* for hours or days awaiting parts, the survivor unmirrored, and ONTAP survives a survivor crash there only because NVRAM persists across reboot. Persistent log media is mandatory.

**3. Shared disks.** Every shelf is cabled to both controllers; ownership is a soft label per disk; takeover reserves the partner's disks; mailbox disks on shared storage carry HA state. Node-local disks are not an HA pair. NetApp's commodity answer is ONTAP Select: aggregates SyncMirror'd across both nodes' local disks over Ethernet, a mediator for the 2-node case, 50% usable, I believe with the NVLOG emulated on local SSD. The brief's assumption is a mistake unless it becomes the Select model; Storage's revised "physical CP-segment shipping" is SyncMirror by another name and the right shape, and identical geometry on both nodes is its price, as it is for plexes.

**4. CP triggers.** The 10 s timer is a ceiling. CPs also fire on NVLOG-half-full, buffer high-water, snapshot and back-to-back; sysstat reports the reason per CP. Under sustained load the interval is log size divided by ingest, often seconds, and CPs run continuously. "One massive contiguous stripe" is the fresh-array case: WAFL writes full stripes into whatever free stripes allocation finds, which is why reallocate and segment cleaning exist. Fine as a simplification, a mistake if the log is sized for 15 s.

**5. Where the ack happens.** In the D-blade after local log and partner mirror complete; the N-blade forwards. The plan matches. Nuance: the mirror goes to the D-blade's HA partner, which is the N-blade's node only in a 2-node cluster.

**6. What the HA interconnect carries.** NVLOG mirror, HA heartbeats, takeover coordination, over InfiniBand, the chassis backplane, or (I believe on newer AFFs) Ethernet RDMA. Never SpinNP, never RDB. The brief's "Data Plane & Write Cache (SpinNP replacement)" fuses two fabrics: SpinNP is N-to-D RPC over the separate cluster network. The specialists split them.

**7. MetroCluster and FC-VI.** FC-VI is a VI (RDMA-style) protocol in purpose-built adapter firmware (I believe QLogic-based NetApp parts) carrying the NVLOG mirror to the DR partner. Data is not "replicated over FC": the owner writes both SyncMirror plexes itself, the remote one through the fabric and FC-to-SAS bridges; the DR node applies nothing. NetApp then built MetroCluster IP (I believe iWARP for the log, iSCSI for remote disks). The brief copied the cable, not the mechanism. Mistake; the specialists' demotion of FC is right.

**8. RDB.** Replicates configuration only: VLDB (volume to aggregate to node), VifMgr (LIF placement), management, SAN config, by majority with epsilon; a 2-node cluster uses "cluster HA" on the HA mailbox mechanism, not a witness. RDB neither decides takeover nor prevents data split-brain; the HA subsystem, reservations and mailbox disks do. Making Raft the arbiter is legitimate (the Pacemaker/SBD shape; Select's mediator is the same idea), provided everyone accepts that without shared disks the fence is lease-plus-watchdog, weaker than a drive refusing a write.

**9. N-blade to D-blade.** VLDB lookups cached in the N-blade, SpinNP sessions via CSM over the cluster network; any LIF serves any volume. SAN LIFs never migrate: partner paths are already logged in as Active/Non-Optimized and takeover flips ALUA state, with no login or rediscovery. Active/Standby is a legitimate Phase-1 simplification forced by LIO; the floating-IP/IQN reinstatement is 7-mode's design, which clustered ONTAP abandoned. Do not build it.

**10. Takeover fencing.** Three liveness channels (interconnect, mailbox disks, Service Processor push via hardware-assisted takeover), then ownership change plus reservations on the partner's disks, replay, aggregates online. The failed node, on reboot, cannot reserve its disks and parks in "waiting for giveback". The plan's ladder is equivalent where a JBOD exists; make iDRAC a push source as HWAT is, not only a ForceOff target.

#### Hard lessons
- **Back-to-back CPs and watchdog storms.** When a CP outlasts the fill time of the other log half, writes suspend until it finishes. Any mechanism that resets a node for lack of I/O progress (I believe ONTAP's protection timers did, on unresponsive disks) hands the load to a partner on the same spindles, which then stalls too. Consensus's "pet the watchdog only while I/O counters advance" rebuilds exactly this: a hung disk resets a healthy node, the survivor inherits the hang and double the load, and resets. Pet on control-plane liveness; alarm on stalled I/O.
- **Log size follows CP duration, not burst ambitions.** ONTAP kept NVRAM small (tens of GB at most; inferring exact sizes) because replay time is takeover time, and a half must drain before the other fills. Rule: half = ingest x max CP duration, record-count capped. The 45-80 s "burst absorption" promise is one ONTAP never made, for this reason.
- **Fragmentation.** Write-anywhere makes random writes sequential and, over months, sequential reads random. Overwrite-heavy LUNs needed scheduled reallocate, read reallocation, free-space reallocation and later continuous segment cleaning; aggregates past ~85-90% full hit a cliff. Every benchmark in the plan runs on a fresh array.
- **Hung disks stall whole stripes.** Full-stripe writes need every member; a disk that neither completes nor fails blocks the CP. ONTAP (I believe) used short per-command timeouts, failed slow disks, copied suspects to spares pre-failure, and tracked per-disk error counters. md waits on SCSI error handling (30 s timeout, escalating resets), SATA drives without SCT ERC retry internally for up to two minutes, and md sees errors, never latency.
- **Write-cache-full is the steady state**, not an edge case, with 12 HDDs behind 10GbE. ONTAP's binary suspend produced the CP latency spikes every support engineer knows; build a proportional throttle on day one.
- **Dual failures.** "In takeover" is a normal state; the survivor is unmirrored; a second failure there is an outage rather than data loss only because NVRAM persists. RAID-DP was born from second disks failing during multi-day rebuilds of large SATA drives; RAID6 covers it, but plan for day-long rebuilds competing with CPs.
- **Partner-down versus partner-unreachable.** Interconnect loss with a live partner means "takeover disabled, unsynchronized log, keep serving", decided by mailbox disks and the SP, never by one silent link. Without shared disks you have only the witness and iDRAC; both must work before any automatic takeover.
- **Giveback failures.** Giveback needs a final CP of the partner's aggregates, release of reservations, and the returning node discarding its stale log; subsystems veto it because a forced giveback disrupts clients. Auto-giveback timers surprised more customers than they helped.
- **"Stateless" front ends.** Every protocol brought state that had to outlive the N-blade: SMB durable and persistent handles, NFSv4 state and grace, iSCSI ISIDs, PRs, stable TPG IDs. Years per protocol; the specialists found the SAN half.
- **RAID and file system were co-designed for a reason.** WAFL checksums carry block identity (I believe), and a mismatch is rebuilt from parity. Under an LFS, md faithfully returns the stale block the map CRC rejects, and nothing lets you ask md for the parity-reconstructed version. Detection without repair is the price of "mdadm as one device".

#### Copy this
- **NVLOG quartering**: two halves per CP, local and partner regions, replay-only semantics, a log generation in the header so a stale log is refused at boot. Cheap: the record and ring already frozen, sized by the rule above.
- **CP trigger taxonomy and sysstat-style counters**: timer, log-full, high-water, back-to-back, duration, bytes, stripes, log fill. Cheap: counters plus a one-line-per-second log.
- **Takeover-disabled, unsynchronized-log** as a first-class alarmed state instead of fencing a healthy partner (Consensus's grant).
- **Hardware-assisted takeover**: subscribe to the partner iDRAC's Redfish or SNMP power and reset events so a crash is pushed within a second; keep ForceOff as the pulled rung.
- **The ONTAP Select HA model** if disks stay local: plex mirroring at CP-segment level, mediator, declared 50% efficiency. Cheap because segment shipping is sequential and the LFS already has CP sequence numbers.
- **Software disk ownership and self-describing aggregates**: owner epoch in the superblock, assemble only after reservation, "waiting for giveback" at boot.
- **Planned takeover first, nightly rehearsal**: final CP, flip, no replay; exercises everything but the fence ladder.
- **Rapid RAID recovery**: md's `want_replacement` copies a suspect member to a spare before failing it; per-member latency and error SLOs in the daemon; SCT ERC at 7 s; SCSI timeout near 10 s.
- **Fullness guardrails**: refuse allocation past ~90%, reserve for cleaning, alarm at 80%.
- **Host Utilities instead of heroics**: ship multipath.conf, ESXi claim rules, Windows MPIO settings and an interop matrix with tuned timeouts. NetApp tunes hosts rather than betting on stock timers.
- **Scrub with identity**: periodic verification of map CRCs with rewrite of failures while parity still has a chance.

#### Do not copy
- **FC as the interconnect.** FC-VI needed custom adapter firmware and NetApp itself left it for Ethernet RDMA. Buy 25GbE RoCE; keep FC, if at all, for distance or FC clients.
- **Your own RAID-DP and 520-byte-sector checksums.** Decades of co-design. Take md, accept detection-without-repair, write metadata twice, return medium errors rather than stale data.
- **The full clustered-ONTAP scale-out machinery**: five RDB units, CSM, indirect access, volume move, epsilon. Takeover is intra-pair; v1 is one pair.
- **7-mode identity takeover** (partner IPs and target IQN re-hosted on the survivor). Pre-stage standby paths instead.
- **Panic-on-slow-I/O.** It assumes a partner on the same shelves with headroom; here it is an outage. Throttle and alarm.
- **Maximal NVRAM.** Do not chase 192 GB of end-of-life NVDIMM-N; a pair of PLP NVMe logs or a small NVDIMM with short CPs is the ONTAP shape.
- **Feature surface in v1**: snapshots, clones, dedup, compression, SnapMirror, NAS. Each touches the CP and the cleaner.
- **Mailbox disks and SP tricks without shared storage.** They do not translate; the witness is the substitute.

#### Phase 1 advice
**Decide before code**: shared SAS shelf (ONTAP's HA model) or the ONTAP Select model (plex mirroring over Ethernet with a mediator, 50% capacity). Either is honest; node-local disks with only a log mirror is neither, and Phase 1's takeover test cannot pass on it.

**Build**: the frozen record and slot ring; local persistence on devdax or PLP NVMe with a loud DRAM prototype flag; the TCP mirror with lease-at-ack; replay; Storage's CP-lite on a real 12-drive md array with the superblock-in-a-stripe commit; the ublk/LIO front door on Rocky 10; planned takeover and giveback (final CP, flip, stale-log discard) driven by an ownership stub. Instrument CPs and log fill the way sysstat does, from the first commit.

**Size and throttle**: log half = ingest x measured max CP duration, record-count capped so replay fits the takeover budget; proportional backpressure at the watermarks; back-to-back CPs are the expected regime, so measure under them.

**Break it early**: hung-disk injection (dm-delay, dm-flakey, scsi_debug) in week one; SCT ERC and SCSI timeouts in the install profile; watchdog petting decoupled from I/O progress; a single-failure test that includes "survivor crashes while in takeover".

**Age before measuring**: fill to 80%, random-overwrite for hours, then take every latency and throughput number.

**Do not attempt**: FC in any form, Raft itself (keep the epoch, lease and watchdog seeds), NFS/SMB, snapshots, cross-node data paths, zero-copy, 1GbE, floating IPs, PR survival, automatic unplanned takeover in product code (spikes of the fence rungs are fine). Unplanned takeover belongs after the witness and the iDRAC push path exist; shipping it earlier is how a storage product earns its first data-loss escalation.
